### 4. **Mental Health and Wellness**

This theme includes coverage of mental health issues, including the prevalence of mental illness, access to mental health services, and community responses to mental health concerns. Important stakeholders in this theme include mental health professionals, advocacy groups, and community organizations. A story like [A single candlelight can chase darkness away](news/193E8287D60F81B8) (September 6, 2023) highlights the importance of faith and community in promoting mental wellness.

### 5. **Community Health and Social Determinants**

This theme encompasses issues related to the social determinants of health, including poverty, education, and housing, as well as community-level initiatives to promote health and well-being. Key institutions in this theme include community organizations, social service agencies, and healthcare providers. For example, a story on the [exhibit showcasing the charm and beauty of Delmarva](news/199F599808CAC350) (June 26, 2024) might touch on the importance of promoting community pride and social cohesion.

**Key People and Institutions**
------------------------------

A range of individuals and organizations play important roles in shaping the Health and Public Safety beat in Easton and the Eastern Shore. These include:

* Healthcare providers, such as hospitals and medical research centers
* Law enforcement agencies, including local police departments and the Talbot County Sheriff's Office
* Social service organizations, such as the Talbot County Department of Social Services

For instance, individuals like Talbot County State's Attorney Joseph Coale and experts like the medical director of Pediatric Urgent Care Services at Johns Hopkins Hospital play crucial roles in shaping the narrative around health and public safety issues.

**Geographic Context**

The Eastern Shore of Maryland, including Talbot County and surrounding areas, presents a unique set of challenges and opportunities for Health and Public Safety coverage. Understanding the geographic context is crucial for effective coverage of this beat. # Local Government, Health, and Public Safety Reporting Guide: Eastern Shore, Maryland

## Introduction
As a reporter covering local government, health, and public safety in Eastern Shore, Maryland, you will be tasked with providing in-depth coverage of the complex issues shaping the region. This guide will serve as a comprehensive resource to help you navigate the intricacies of the beat, providing context, insights, and expert advice to aid in your reporting.

### Understanding the Beat Landscape
The Eastern Shore of Maryland encompasses a diverse range of counties, including Caroline, Kent, Queen Anne's, Talbot, Dorchester, Wicomico, Somerset, and Worcester. Each county has its unique character, challenges, and opportunities, making it essential to understand the local dynamics.

To effectively cover this beat, you will need to be familiar with the key stakeholders, including local government officials, law enforcement agencies, healthcare providers, and community organizations. Building relationships with these sources will be crucial in gathering information and providing insightful reporting.

Some of the key issues to focus on in your reporting include:

* Local government decisions and policies
* Public safety concerns, such as crime rates and law enforcement strategies
* Healthcare access and quality in the region
* Community development initiatives and economic growth

### Major Themes in Local Government, Health, and Public Safety Reporting
Our analysis of recent news stories has identified several major themes that consistently appear in coverage of the Eastern Shore:

1. **Economic Development and Tourism**: The Eastern Shore is known for its natural beauty, rich history, and vibrant culture, making it a popular tourist destination. Stories often focus on efforts to boost local economies through tourism initiatives, such as the development of waterfront properties, festivals, and events.
2. **Public Safety and Law Enforcement**: Crime and public safety are ongoing concerns in the region. Coverage often highlights the efforts of local law enforcement agencies to address these issues, including community policing initiatives and crime prevention programs.
3. **Healthcare Access and Quality**: The Eastern Shore faces unique healthcare challenges, including limited access to specialized care and a high prevalence of certain health conditions. Reporting on healthcare issues, such as hospital closures, medical staffing shortages, and community health initiatives, is essential.
4. **Environmental Concerns**: The region's natural resources, including the Chesapeake Bay and its tributaries, are a significant focus of local reporting. Stories often cover environmental issues, such as water quality, conservation efforts, and the impact of climate change.
5. **Community Development and Social Issues**: The Eastern Shore is home to a diverse range of communities, each with its own unique challenges and opportunities. Reporting on community development initiatives, social issues, such as poverty and education, and the impact of policy decisions on local residents is vital.

### Key People and Institutions
To effectively cover the Eastern Shore, you will need to be familiar with the key people and institutions that shape the region. Some of the key players include:

* Local government officials, such as county commissioners and mayors
* Law enforcement agencies, including police departments and sheriff's offices
* Healthcare providers, such as hospitals and medical centers
* Community organizations, including non-profits and advocacy groups
* Business leaders and economic development organizations

### Geographic Context
The Eastern Shore's geography plays a significant role in shaping the region's issues and challenges. The region's rural nature, coastal location, and agricultural heritage all contribute to its unique character. Understanding the geographic context will help you to better appreciate the complexities of the issues and provide more nuanced reporting.

### Sources to Build Relationships With
To gather information and provide insightful reporting, you will need to build relationships with a range of sources, including:

* Local government officials and their staff
* Law enforcement agencies and officers
* Healthcare providers and professionals
* Community leaders and activists
* Business owners and economic development professionals

### Data and Public Records to Track
To provide in-depth coverage, you will need to track a range of data and public records, including:

* Local government budgets and financial reports
* Crime statistics and law enforcement data
* Healthcare data, such as hospital admissions and health outcomes
* Environmental data, such as water quality and air quality reports

### Proposing Story Ideas
Some potential story ideas to consider include:

* Investigating the impact of tourism on local economies and communities
* Examining the effectiveness of community policing initiatives in reducing crime
* Reporting on healthcare access and quality issues in the region
* Covering environmental concerns, such as the impact of climate change on the Chesapeake Bay
* Profiling community development initiatives and social issues, such as poverty and education

By understanding the complexities of the Eastern Shore and building relationships with key sources, you will be well-equipped to provide insightful and informative reporting on the region's most pressing issues.

## Training Guide for Health and Public Safety Reporting in Easton and the Eastern Shore

### Introduction

As a reporter covering health and public safety in Easton and the broader Eastern Shore, you will be tasked with understanding the complex issues and institutions that shape the region. This guide will provide you with a comprehensive overview of the landscape, stakeholders, and incentives that drive the beat.

The Eastern Shore of Maryland encompasses a diverse geographic area, including the counties of Caroline, Dorchester, Kent, Queen Anne's, Talbot, and others. The region is characterized by its rural landscape, scenic waterways, and tight-knit communities.

Health and public safety issues are critical concerns in the region, with topics ranging from access to healthcare and social services to emergency response and crime prevention. As a reporter, you will need to navigate a complex web of stakeholders, including government agencies, non-profit organizations, and community groups.

### Key Themes

After analyzing the provided dataset of news stories, we have identified several key themes that consistently appear in coverage of health and public safety in the Eastern Shore:

1. **Access to Healthcare**: Stories about healthcare access, quality, and affordability are common in the region. For example, a story from September 15, 2023, highlights a cooking class at the St. Michaels Community Center, which provides a unique approach to health education. [1]
2. **Community Events and Initiatives**: Community events, such as health fairs, charity runs, and awareness campaigns, are frequently covered in local news. A story from December 13, 2024, reports on a fundraiser for the Caroline North Little League, showcasing community engagement and support. [2]
3. **Crime and Public Safety**: Crime stories, including arrests, investigations, and court proceedings, are a regular feature of local news. A story from February 12, 2025, reports on a shooting incident in Greensboro, highlighting the need for effective law enforcement and community resources. [3]
4. **Emergency Response and Preparedness**: News stories often focus on emergency response efforts, including natural disasters, accidents, and medical emergencies. A story from November 14, 2024, discusses voter turnout and election results, which can impact public safety and health initiatives. [4]
5. **Social Services and Support**: Coverage of social services, including food banks, homeless shelters, and other support organizations, is common in the region. A story from October 20, 2023, highlights various religious events and services, demonstrating the importance of faith communities in providing support. [5]

### Key People and Institutions

To effectively cover health and public safety in the Eastern Shore, you will need to understand the key people and institutions involved in these issues. Some of the most important stakeholders include:

1. **Local Government Agencies**: County and municipal government agencies, such as health departments, law enforcement, and emergency management offices, play a critical role in shaping health and public safety policies and initiatives.
2. **Healthcare Providers**: Hospitals, clinics, and other healthcare providers are essential to the region's healthcare infrastructure. For example, a story from June 14, 2025, discusses the impact of a Trump administration policy on science research and healthcare. [6]
3. **Non-Profit Organizations**: Non-profit organizations, such as the American Red Cross, the Salvation Army, and local advocacy groups, provide critical services and support to vulnerable populations.
4. **Community Groups**: Community groups, including faith organizations, civic associations, and advocacy organizations, are vital to promoting health and public safety initiatives.

### Geographic Context

The Eastern Shore's unique geography, including its rural landscape, waterways, and coastal communities, shapes the region's health and public safety issues. For example:

1. **Rural Health Disparities**: The region's rural nature can create challenges for accessing healthcare services, particularly in more remote areas.
2. **Coastal Hazards**: The Eastern Shore's coastal location makes it vulnerable to natural hazards, such as hurricanes, flooding, and storm surges.
3. **Waterborne Illnesses**: The region's waterways and aquatic activities can pose health risks, including waterborne illnesses.

### Sources and Data

To effectively cover health and public safety in the Eastern Shore, you will need to cultivate a range of sources, including:

1. **Government Officials**: Building relationships with government officials, such as health department directors, law enforcement officials, and emergency management personnel, can provide valuable insights and information.
2. **Healthcare Professionals**: Healthcare providers, including doctors, nurses, and other medical professionals, can offer expert perspectives on health issues and trends.
3. **Community Leaders**: Community leaders, including faith leaders, civic organization leaders, and advocacy group representatives, can provide context and insights on community concerns and initiatives.

Some key data sources to track include:

1. **Health Department Data**: Local health department data on disease incidence, health trends, and program outcomes can provide valuable insights.
2. **Crime Statistics**: Crime statistics, including crime rates and arrest data, can help inform public safety coverage.
3. **Emergency Management Data**: Emergency management data, including disaster response and recovery efforts, can provide critical information on the region's preparedness and response.

### Story Ideas

Some potential story ideas to explore include:

1. **Access to Healthcare in Rural Areas**: Investigate the challenges and opportunities for improving healthcare access in rural areas of the Eastern Shore. [7]
2. **Community-Based Initiatives**: Highlight community-based initiatives that promote health and public safety, such as health fairs, fitness programs, and crime prevention efforts.
3. **Emergency Preparedness**: Examine the region's emergency preparedness efforts, including disaster response plans, emergency management training, and community outreach initiatives.

By understanding the key themes, people, institutions, and geographic context that shape health and public safety in the Eastern Shore, you can develop a nuanced and comprehensive understanding of the issues and produce high-quality reporting that informs and engages the community.

# Training Guide – Health & Public‑Safety Reporting on the Eastern Shore  
*Prepared for early‑career reporters joining the **Star‑Democrat** newsroom*  

---

## 1.  Getting Started – What This Beat Looks Like  

You already know how to write a story, conduct an interview, and meet a deadline. This guide will help you **learn the people, institutions, and local dynamics that shape health and public‑safety coverage in East Easton and the broader Eastern Shore**.  

*The focus is forward‑looking*: you will be asked to spot emerging threats, follow up on unfinished investigations, and explain how policy decisions will affect the community in the months and years ahead. Historical context is provided only when it clarifies why a current issue matters.  

---  

### 1.1  Core Coverage Areas  

| Topic | Why It Matters | Typical Sources | Recent Example (link) |
|-------|----------------|----------------|-----------------------|
| **Police oversight & accountability** | Public trust, use‑of‑force, civilian‑review processes | Police Accountability Board, Sheriff’s Office, Trial Board, state attorney general, community groups | *Caroline Police Accountability Board reports progress* (Feb 2024) – [link](https://star-dem.com/news/19738B6970702968) |
| **Gun violence & shootings** | Immediate threat to life, ripple effects on emergency services | Police incident reports, sheriff’s office, EMS, hospitals, victims’ families | *Police investigating shooting in Greensboro* (Feb 2025) – [link](https://star-dem.com/news/19EB92016F693118) |
| **Human‑trafficking & exploitation** | Hidden crime that endangers vulnerable populations | For All Seasons, local NGOs, law‑enforcement task forces, shelters | *For All Seasons gives update on human trafficking in Talbot County* (Jan 2025) – [link](https://star-dem.com/news/19E732FE924F9770) |
| **Substance‑abuse & opioid crisis** | Overdose deaths, strain on EMS & hospitals | Health department, addiction treatment centers, EMS run‑sheets, coroners | (still under‑reported – opportunity) |
| **School safety & inclusive policies** | Student well‑being, community tension over “Safe Space” signs | School boards, superintendents, teachers, parent groups, ACLU | *Removal of Safe Space stickers draws criticism* (May 2025) – [link](https://star-dem.com/news/1A0A23BEEF50B390) |
| **Licensing & regulatory health impacts** (liquor, cannabis, wind‑energy) | Alcohol‑related injuries, environmental health, tax revenue | County liquor board, Maryland Energy Administration, health department | *Talbot County Liquor Board license applications available* (Jan 2025) – [link](https://star-dem.com/news/19E09ACFD858B638) |
| **Community health planning** (advanced directives, hospice) | Aging population, end‑of‑life decisions, health‑system capacity | Talbot Hospice, health‑care providers, state health commissioner | *Advanced Healthcare Planning* events (Feb 2024) – see calendar listings |

---  

## 2.  Themes That Keep Emerging  

Below are the **significant, repeatable themes** that appear in the newsroom’s recent archive. For each theme you’ll find:  

* **Significance** – why readers care.  
* **Key institutions** that shape the story.  
* **Important stakeholders** – who is speaking, who is affected.  
* **Competing viewpoints** – the “sides” you’ll need to balance.  
* **Typical situations** where the theme surfaces.  
* **Recent story(s)** that illustrate the theme (with month / year and hyperlink).  

### 2.1  Police Accountability & Civilian Oversight  

* **Significance** – The Eastern Shore’s small‑town policing model relies on community confidence. Recent reforms (Maryland Police Accountability Act of 2021) have created new boards that audit complaints and recommend discipline.  
* **Key institutions** – Caroline County Police Accountability Board, the Administrative Charging Committee, the Trial Board (Judge Owen Wise), Maryland Police Training & Standards Commission, Maryland Police Accountability Act.  
* **Stakeholders** – County commissioners, police chiefs (e.g., **John Bartlett**, **Kimberly Raeder**), community activists, victims of alleged misconduct, the state attorney general.  
* **Competing viewpoints** – Police argue that the process is “burdensome” and threatens officer safety; advocates claim it is essential for transparency.  
* **Typical situations** – Release of annual reports, high‑profile use‑of‑force complaints, hearings on body‑camera activation.  
* **Recent example** – *Caroline Police Accountability Board reports progress* (Feb 2024) – the board disclosed only six complaints out of > 26,000 calls for service, noting that five were exonerated. The story notes that “body‑camera activation remains a trend” (unresolved). – [link](https://star-dem.com/news/19738B6970702968)

> **Caveat** – The board’s recommendations (e.g., moving training online) are still pending County Commission approval; check the latest minutes before reporting.  

### 2.2  Gun Violence & Active‑Shooter Incidents  

* **Significance** – Even isolated shootings create fear, affect EMS response times, and raise questions about gun‑policy enforcement in a largely rural region.  
* **Key institutions** – Caroline County Sheriff’s Office, local police departments (Greensboro PD, Easton PD), emergency‑medical services, state police.  
* **Stakeholders** – Victims and families, gun‑rights groups, public‑safety advocates, local legislators.  
* **Competing viewpoints** – Calls for stricter gun‑storage laws versus Second‑Amendment rights.  
* **Typical situations** – Police press releases after an incident, community town‑hall meetings, court hearings on firearm permits.  
* **Recent example** – *Police investigating shooting in Greensboro* (Feb 2025) – a parking‑lot shooting at a Save‑A‑Lot; investigation ongoing, suspect not yet identified. – [link](https://star-dem.com/news/19EB92016F693118)  

> **Caveat** – No arrests have been made; the status may change as investigators release updates.  

### 2.3  Human‑Trafficking & Exploitation  

* **Significance** – The Eastern Shore’s proximity to ports and agricultural labor markets makes it a corridor for trafficking, especially of women and minors.  
* **Key institutions** – For All Seasons (regional anti‑trafficking nonprofit), Talbot County Council, Maryland Attorney General’s Office, local law‑enforcement task forces.  
* **Stakeholders** – Survivors, service providers, law‑enforcement, community advocates, faith‑based groups.  
* **Competing viewpoints** – Law‑enforcement focuses on criminal prosecution; NGOs stress victim‑centered services and prevention education.  
* **Typical situations** – Council briefings, public awareness trainings, court cases against traffickers, grant‑funding announcements.  
* **Recent example** – *For All Seasons gives update on human trafficking in Talbot County* (Jan 2025) – council hearing, noting a “double‑edged sword” in reporting trends; the organization has delivered an online anti‑trafficking course to 120 participants. – [link](https://star-dem.com/news/19E732FE924F9770)

> **Caveat** – The “double‑edged sword” comment signals that the problem is ongoing; follow up on any pending prosecutions or new legislative proposals.  

### 2.4  School‑Safety Policies & “Safe Space” Controversy  

* **Significance** – Policies meant to protect LGBTQ+ students have become flashpoints for broader cultural debates, affecting school climate and parental trust.  
* **Key institutions** – Calvert County Board of Education, Superintendent **Andra Townsel**, school‑district legal counsel, parent‑advocacy groups, the **Beau Biden Foundation** (which supplied the original presentation).  
* **Stakeholders** – Students, teachers, parents, LGBTQ+ advocacy groups, conservative community organizations.  
* **Competing viewpoints** – Pro‑inclusion supporters argue stickers signal safety; opponents claim they are “propaganda” and cite a single criminal case as justification.  
* **Typical situations** – Board meetings where stickers are removed, public comment periods, media coverage of protests.  
* **Recent example** – *Removal of Safe Space stickers draws criticism* (May 2025) – Board member **Melissa Goshorn** requested removal after a “bone‑chilling” presentation about a principal’s arrest; the decision sparked 19 of 20 public speakers at the next board meeting. – [link](https://star-dem.com/news/1A0A23BEEF50B390)

> **Caveat** – The board later voted to discuss a flag‑policy that could affect future signage; monitor the policy’s final wording.  

### 2.5  Licensing & Regulation (Liquor, Cannabis, Wind Energy)  

* **Significance** – Licensing decisions influence public‑health outcomes (e.g., DUI rates, alcohol‑related injuries) and economic development (jobs, tax revenue).  
* **Key institutions** – Talbot County Liquor Board, Maryland Energy Administration, Chesapeake Bay Maritime Museum (for the wind‑energy project), County Planning & Zoning Commissions.  
* **Stakeholders** – Business owners, residents, health‑department officials, environmental groups, law‑enforcement (for enforcement).  
* **Competing viewpoints** – Economic‑development proponents vs. public‑health advocates wary of increased alcohol or cannabis availability.  
* **Typical situations** – Publication of licensing applications, public‑notice hearings, environmental impact statements, council debates.  
* **Recent example** – *Talbot County Liquor Board license applications available* (Jan 2025) – the board posted the application portal and reminded applicants of protest‑filing deadlines. – [link](https://star-dem.com/news/19E09ACFD858B638)  

> **Caveat** – The “protest” clause may lead to legal challenges; check court filings for any pending lawsuits.  

---  

## 3.  People You’ll Meet Frequently  

Below is a **narrative portrait** of the individuals who appear repeatedly in health‑ and safety‑related coverage. Knowing who they are and how they are likely to respond will streamline your reporting.  

| Person | Role & Relevance | Typical Appearances | How to Approach |
|--------|------------------|---------------------|-----------------|
| **John Bartlett** – Chair, Caroline Police Accountability Board | Leads the civilian oversight body; often the public face of the board’s annual reports. | *Caroline Police Accountability Board reports progress* (Feb 2024) – quoted on complaint statistics. | Request a one‑on‑one interview after board meetings; bring specific data points (e.g., body‑camera activation). |
| **Kimberly Raeder** – Board Administrator (same board) | Provides procedural background, explains statutory mandates. | Same as Bartlett; also discusses training requirements. | Use as a “policy‑expert” source for the Maryland Police Accountability Act. |
| **Christopher Morgan** – Subject of the Greensboro shooting | A civilian victim; his case illustrates the human impact of gun violence. | *Police investigating shooting in Greensboro* (Feb 2025). | Contact his family via the sheriff’s office liaison; be sensitive to trauma. |
| **George Bacorn** – Chief, Greensboro Police Department | Provides law‑enforcement perspective on investigations. | Same as Morgan. | Request an official statement on investigation status; ask about inter‑agency cooperation. |
| **Owen Wise** – Retired Circuit Court Judge, Trial Board member | Decides disciplinary outcomes for police officers; his opinions carry legal weight. | Mentioned in the Police Accountability Board article. | Approach through the Board’s public‑information officer; ask about precedent‑setting decisions. |
| **Melissa Goshorn** – Calvert County Board of Education member | Influential in school‑policy debates; spearheaded removal of Safe‑Space stickers. | *Removal of Safe Space stickers draws criticism* (May 2025). | Offer to quote her on policy rationale; ask about future “flag” policy plans. |
| **Andra Townsel** – Superintendent, Calvert County Schools | Oversees district‑wide safety and inclusion policies; author of the Safe‑Space decision. | Same as Goshorn. | Ask about data on bullying incidents and how signage impacts student well‑being. |
| **Lauren Weber** – Vice President, Strategy & Development, For All Seasons | Leads anti‑trafficking outreach; familiar with law‑enforcement partnerships. | *For All Seasons gives update* (Jan 2025). | Request statistics on referrals; ask about gaps in victim services. |
| **Katie Theeke** – Vice President, Marketing & Communications, For All Seasons | Handles public‑awareness campaigns; can speak to community education efforts. | Same as Weber. | Ask about upcoming trainings for school staff. |
| **Andy Harris** – U.S. Representative (MD‑1st) | Frequently quoted on public‑safety funding, law‑enforcement oversight, and community‑development projects. | *Harris speaks to Dorchester Chamber* (Mar 2025). | Contact his district office for statements on federal safety grants. |
| **Marc Watson** – Supervisor of Health, PE & Athletics, Chesapeake College | Oversees physical‑education curricula; relevant when reporting on youth health and school‑sports safety. | *New director takes the helm at Chesapeake’s Cambridge Center* (Aug 2023). | Ask about community‑college health initiatives (e.g., concussion protocols). |
| **Victoria Bradley** – Secretary, Talbot County Liquor Board | Provides guidance on licensing procedures; often quoted on protest deadlines. | *Talbot County Liquor Board license applications available* (Jan 2025). | Ask for statistics on liquor‑license density vs. DUI citations. |

---  

## 4.  Institutions – The Power Structures You’ll Navigate  

| Institution | What It Does | Why It Matters for Health & Safety | Typical Interaction with the Beat |
|------------|--------------|-----------------------------------|-----------------------------------|
| **Caroline County Police Accountability Board (PAB)** | Civilian board that receives complaints, recommends policy, and forwards cases to the Administrative Charging Committee. | Sets standards for police conduct; influences community trust and potential litigation. | Publishes annual reports; holds public meetings; coordinates with the Trial Board. |
| **Administrative Charging Committee (ACC)** | Reviews completed investigations and determines disciplinary actions using a state‑wide matrix. | Directly decides whether officers face reprimands, suspensions, or termination. | Works with PAB; its decisions are often appealed in state courts. |
| **Trial Board** (incl. **Judge Owen Wise**) | Final adjudicative body for officer discipline; can impose sanctions. | Provides a legal check on the ACC; outcomes are public record. | Meets quarterly; publishes rulings on the county website. |
| **Caroline County Sheriff’s Office** | Primary law‑enforcement agency; conducts investigations, arrests, and community policing. | Generates the bulk of crime‑statistics that shape public‑safety narratives. | Issues press releases; provides data to the PAB and media. |
| **Calvert County Board of Education** | Governs public‑school policy, including safety, inclusion, and student‑wellness initiatives. | Decisions on “Safe Space” signage, anti‑bullying programs, and health‑curriculum affect thousands of students. | Holds monthly public meetings; works with the superintendent. |
| **For All Seasons (FAS)** | Regional anti‑trafficking nonprofit that provides victim services, training, and advocacy. | Operates the primary referral network for trafficking victims in the Mid‑Shore. | Reports to county councils; partners with law‑enforcement task forces. |
| **Talbot County Liquor Board** | Issues and renews liquor licenses; enforces local alcohol‑sale regulations. | Licensing density correlates with DUI crashes and alcohol‑related hospitalizations. | Publishes applications online; holds hearings for contested renewals. |
| **Maryland Energy Administration (MEA)** | Oversees offshore wind projects; approves permits and monitors environmental impact. | Wind‑farm construction may affect air‑quality and marine‑ecosystem health, influencing local health outcomes. | Coordinates with the National Park Service and local ports. |
| **Talbot Hospice & Local Health‑Care Providers** (e.g., **Johns Hopkins Hospital**, **University of Maryland Medical Center**) | Provide end‑of‑life care, advanced‑directive counseling, and acute medical services. | Their data reveal trends in chronic disease, overdose, and aging‑population needs. | Host community workshops; publish health‑statistics. |
| **County Planning & Zoning Commissions** (Talbot, Caroline, Dorchester) | Review land‑use proposals that can affect public health (e.g., new liquor‑store locations, cannabis dispensaries). | Zoning decisions dictate where potentially risky businesses locate, influencing community exposure. | Hold public hearings; accept protests from residents. |
| **Local Media Outlets & Community Organizations** (e.g., **Kent Cultural Alliance**, **Easton Chamber of Commerce**) | Amplify public‑safety messages, organize town‑halls, and curate cultural events that impact mental health. | Offer additional sources of community sentiment and event calendars. | Partner for story‑tips; attend their events. |

---  

## 5.  Geographic Context – How Place Shapes Health & Safety  

The Eastern Shore’s **rural‑coastal geography** creates a distinctive backdrop for health and public‑safety reporting.  

| Geographic Feature | Impact on Coverage | Example Locations from the Archive |
|--------------------|--------------------|-------------------------------------|
| **Scattered, low‑density towns** (e.g., **Easton**, **Cambridge**, **Denton**) | Longer EMS response times; limited specialty‑care facilities; reliance on regional hospitals. | *Police investigating shooting in Greensboro* (Greensboro is a small unincorporated community in Caroline County). |
| **Coastal waterways & ports** (Chesapeake Bay, Miles River, Nanticoke River) | Facilitates trafficking routes, creates occupational hazards for watermen, and influences wind‑energy siting. | *Sail Selina II* operates on the Miles River; *Wind‑energy project* located 10 mi off Ocean City. |
| **Agricultural economy** (large farms, processing plants) | Exposure to pesticide‑related illnesses, farm‑worker injuries, and seasonal labor‑migration patterns that affect trafficking risk. | *AquaCon salmon‑farm proposal* (referenced in the “Towns need to balance pride” column). |
| **Transportation corridors** (US‑50, MD‑50, I‑95) | High‑speed traffic corridors raise crash and DUI concerns; also serve as evacuation routes for storms. | *Pedestrian bridge over MD‑50* (cost estimate article, Dec 2024). |
| **Aging population in historic towns** (e.g., **St. Michael’s**, **Oxford**) | Higher demand for hospice, advanced‑directive services, and fall‑prevention programs. | *Advanced Healthcare Planning* sessions held at Talbot Hospice (Feb 2024). |
| **County boundaries that cross cultural lines** (e.g., **Talbot** vs. **Caroline**) | Different local ordinances on alcohol, cannabis, and school policies create “policy patchwork” that reporters must map. | *Safe Space sticker removal* (Calvert County) vs. *Cannabis ordinance* (Easton, Apr 2024). |

When you file a story, always **anchor the health or safety issue to a specific place** – the nearest town, the relevant jurisdiction, and any physical features that affect the outcome.  

---  

## 6.  Building a Source Network  

### 6.1  Core Contacts to Cultivate  

| Category | Who to Meet | How to Reach Them | Frequency of Interaction |
|----------|-------------|-------------------|---------------------------|
| **Law‑Enforcement** | Sheriff’s Office spokesperson, local police chiefs, PAB members (Bartlett, Raeder) | Phone (county‑wide press line), email after board meetings | Weekly (monitor blotters) |
| **Public‑Health** | County health director, hospital liaison officers, hospice directors | Attend health‑department webinars; join County Health Advisory Board meetings | Monthly |
| **Social‑Services & NGOs** | For All Seasons staff (Weber, Theeke), local shelters, crisis‑line operators | Volunteer at awareness events; request data sharing agreements | Quarterly |
| **Education** | Superintendents (Townsel), school‑board members (Goshorn), principals | Attend board meetings; schedule “open‑office” visits | Every board meeting (usually monthly) |
| **Regulatory Bodies** | Liquor Board (Bradley), Planning & Zoning Commissioners, MEA project managers | Submit FOIA requests for licensing data; attend public hearings | As projects arise |
| **Community Leaders** | Chamber of Commerce presidents, faith‑based leaders, senior‑center coordinators | Join local networking groups (e.g., Rotary, Lions) | Bi‑monthly |
| **State Agencies** | Maryland Department of Health, Attorney General’s Office, Maryland Police Training & Standards Commission | Subscribe to state newsletters; request briefings | As policy changes occur |

### 6.2  Data & Public‑Record Sources  

| Record | Where to Find It | What It Shows | Reporting Uses |
|--------|------------------|----------------|----------------|
| **Crime & Incident Logs** | County Sheriff’s Office website, Maryland Open Data Portal | Calls for service, arrest data, location, time | Trend analysis of shootings, drug‑related calls |
| **EMS Run Sheets** | Local EMS agencies (e.g., Easton Ambulance) – request via FOIA | Response times, medical emergencies, overdose incidents | Evaluate impact of traffic or event‑related spikes |
| **Hospital Discharge Data** | Maryland Health Services Cost Review Commission (HSCRC) | ICD‑10 coded diagnoses (e.g., opioid overdose) | Public‑health stories on morbidity trends |
| **Licensing Databases** | County Liquor Board portal, Maryland Cannabis Commission (for future cannabis licensing) | Number of active licenses, renewal status, violations | Correlate with DUI or alcohol‑related injury data |
| **Court Records** | Maryland Judiciary Case Search | Criminal docket information, sentencing, restraining orders | Follow up on ongoing prosecutions (e.g., attempted‑murder case) |
| **School‑Safety Plans** | County Board of Education website, public‑meeting minutes | Policies on bullying, inclusive signage, emergency drills | Context for “Safe Space” or other school‑climate stories |
| **Environmental Impact Statements (EIS)** | Maryland Department of the Environment (MDE) | Air‑quality, noise, water‑quality projections for wind farms | Assess health implications of large infrastructure projects |

### 6.3  Habits for Success  

1. **Morning Blotter Scan** – Open the county Sheriff’s “Daily Activity Report” each morning; note any incidents near your beat.  
2. **Weekly Calendar Review** – Cross‑check the *Mid‑Shore Calendar* (published each week) for public meetings, health fairs, and community events that may generate leads.  
3. **Two‑Source Rule** – For any statistic, obtain at least one official source (e.g., health department) and one community source (e.g., hospital spokesperson).  
4. **Document Everything** – Keep a running spreadsheet of contacts, dates of interviews, and FOIA request IDs; it saves time when a story resurfaces.  
5. **Stay Alert to State‑Level Changes** – When Maryland passes a new health or safety law (e.g., the 2021 Police Accountability Act), note how local agencies are adapting.  

---  

## 7.  Story Ideas – Under‑Covered Angles & Emerging Issues  

Below are **10 story‑pitch ideas** that build on the themes above, include a brief angle description, and suggest sources or locations to investigate. All are framed as **future‑oriented** pieces that can be pursued now.  

| # | Pitch Title | Angle (Why It Matters) | Suggested Sources / Locations | Status of Related Coverage |
|---|-------------|------------------------|------------------------------|----------------------------|
| 1 | *“The Hidden Cost of Alcohol: Mapping DUI Hotspots After New Liquor Licenses”* | Examine whether the 2025–2026 liquor‑license boom correlates with increased DUI crashes in Talbot and surrounding counties. | Talbot Liquor Board (Bradley), Maryland State Police traffic division, local EMS run‑sheet data, hospitals (Easton Medical Center) | License applications announced Jan 2025 – no follow‑up yet. |
| 2 | *“From the Bay to the Bench: How Offshore Wind Construction Affects Respiratory Health”* | Track air‑quality measurements before and after the US Wind project begins, interviewing residents near the construction zone. | Maryland Energy Administration, Chesapeake Bay Maritime Museum, local health‑department air‑monitoring stations, nearby homeowners (St. Michael’s). | Project approved Sep 2024 – construction slated for 2025. |
| 3 | *“Silent Victims: The Rise of Rural Trafficking Calls in Caroline County”* | Use 911 call‑center data to show whether trafficking‑related reports are increasing; compare with For All Seasons’ training statistics. | Caroline County Sheriff’s Office, For All Seasons (Weber, Theeke), state trafficking task‑force. | FAS update Jan 2025 – trend noted but not quantified. |
| 4 | *“Body‑Camera Gaps: Why Some Officers Still Forget to Turn On Their Cameras”* | Investigate the systemic reasons behind missed body‑camera activation identified in the PAB report; interview tech‑training staff. | Police Accountability Board (Bartlett, Raeder), MD Police Training & Standards Commission, officer unions. | PAB report Feb 2024 – trend acknowledged, no concrete plan yet. |
| 5 | *“Safe‑Space Backlash: How Removal of LGBTQ+ Signage Affects Student Mental Health”* | Survey students and counselors at Calvert County schools before and after the sticker removal to assess anxiety levels. | Calvert County Board of Education (Goshorn, Townsel), school counselors, student focus groups. | Sticker removal May 2025 – ongoing debate. |
| 6 | *“The Unseen Toll of Farm Labor: Pesticide Exposure Among Seasonal Workers”* | Partner with a local health‑clinic to test blood‑lead levels in workers at farms that were potential AquaCon sites. | Maryland Department of Agriculture, local farms (e.g., former AquaCon site), Talbot County Health Department. | AquaCon proposal mentioned in 2024 editorial; never realized, but similar farms exist. |
| 7 | *“From Arrest to Recovery: Tracking the Journey of a Federalsburg Shooting Victim”* | Long‑form follow‑up on the 16‑year‑old charged with attempted murder; examine the juvenile justice process and victim services. | Caroline County Juvenile Court, victim‑advocacy groups, school district (if victim is a student). | Shooting reported May 2025 – case still pending. |
| 8 | *“The ‘Flag Policy’ Fallout: Will Future Signage Be Policed?”* | Analyze the new flag‑policy adopted after the Safe‑Space controversy; assess its implications for future school‑safety signage. | Calvert County Board of Education (meeting minutes), legal counsel, ACLU Maryland. | Policy voted on May 2025 – implementation details unknown. |
| 9 | *“Emergency‑Room Overcrowding in Rural Hospitals: A Look at Easton’s Capacity”* | Examine whether the increase in seasonal tourism and wind‑farm construction strains the emergency department of Talbot County’s hospitals. | Talbot Hospice, Easton Medical Center administration, Maryland Health Services Cost Review Commission data. | No recent reporting; data available through HSCRC. |
|10| *“Community‑Based Mental‑Health Crisis Lines: Are They Reaching Rural Youth?”* | Evaluate usage statistics of Maryland’s 988 mental‑health line among teenagers in Dorchester and Queen Anne’s counties; interview school counselors. | Maryland Department of Health, local schools (Easton High, Queen Anne’s County High), crisis‑line operators. | 988 launched nationally in 2022; little local coverage. |

> **Tip:** When you pursue any of these ideas, **verify the current status** (e.g., court outcomes, policy adoption) because many of the stories in the archive are *unfinished* or *pending*.

---  

## 8.  Unfinished Storylines – Where to Start Verification  

| Story (Month Year) | Unresolved Element | What to Check Now |
|--------------------|-------------------|-------------------|
| *Caroline Police Accountability Board reports progress* (Feb 2024) | Implementation of recommendations (online training, body‑camera policy). | Review County Commission meeting minutes from June‑July 2024; request a status update from Board Chair. |
| *For All Seasons gives update on human trafficking* (Jan 2025) | Any prosecutions or new legislation since the council hearing. | Contact the Attorney General’s trafficking unit; ask For All Seasons for recent case counts. |
| *Removal of Safe Space stickers draws criticism* (May 2025) | Final wording of the “flag policy” and any subsequent lawsuits. | Obtain the policy text from Calvert County Board of Education website; search county court docket for any filing. |
| *Police investigating shooting in Greensboro* (Feb 2025) | Identification and arrest of the shooter(s). | Follow the Greensboro Police Department’s press releases; file a FOIA request for the incident report. |
| *Attempted murder in Federalsburg* (May 2025) | Court disposition for the juvenile suspect and the adult accessory. | Check Maryland Courts’ online docket for case number; contact the Caroline County State’s Attorney’s office. |
| *Easton cannabis ordinance* (Apr 2024) | Whether the ordinance has been fully enacted after the Governor’s signature. | Look up the latest Easton Town Council agenda; verify with the Maryland Department of Health licensing division. |
| *Pedestrian bridge over MD‑50* (Dec 2024) | Final location selection and funding status. | Review Queen Anne’s County Commission meeting recordings (Nov‑Dec 2024) and BMC grant award notices. |

---  

## 9.  Quick Reference Checklist  

- **Daily** – Scan police blotter & EMS run‑sheet summary.  
- **Weekly** – Attend at least one public meeting (Board of Education, County Commission, PAB).  
- **Monthly** – Update spreadsheet with new licensing applications, court dockets, and health‑department alerts.  
- **Quarterly** – Request data sets (crime, hospitalizations, licensing) via FOIA; verify any pending storylines.  
- **Bi‑annually** – Conduct a “pulse check” interview with a key stakeholder (e.g., PAB Chair, For All Seasons VP).  

---  

### Final Thought  

Reporting on health and public safety in the Eastern Shore is **about connecting data, policy, and people to the lived reality of a geographically dispersed community**. Your beat will require you to be both a **watchdog** (holding institutions accountable) and a **connector** (showing how a wind‑farm, a liquor license, or a school‑policy decision ripples through families’ health and safety.  

Use the people, institutions, and themes outlined here as a **roadmap**. Keep your sources current, verify the status of ongoing stories, and always ground your reporting in the specific place – the towns, waterways, and roadways that shape daily life on the Eastern Shore.  

Good luck, and welcome to a beat that matters to every resident from **Cambridge** to **St. Michael’s**.  

---  

*Prepared by the Star‑Democrat newsroom, 2025*  

=== prototype_v9.md ===
# Training Guide – Health & Public‑Safety Reporting on the Eastern Shore  
**Audience:** Early‑career reporters assigned to Easton, Talbot County, and the broader Eastern Shore (Maryland).  
**Purpose:** Give you the context, story‑building tools, and daily habits you need to cover health‑related and public‑safety beats effectively.

---

## 1. Why This Beat Matters  

The Eastern Shore is a rural, aging, and increasingly diverse region where health outcomes, safety concerns, and public‑policy intersect in ways that differ sharply from the Baltimore‑Washington corridor. Issues such as opioid misuse, human‑trafficking, flood‑related health hazards, and the rollout of new cannabis regulations have direct consequences for residents’ wellbeing and for the credibility of local institutions. Your reporting can:

* Hold government agencies and service providers accountable.  
* Highlight gaps in health‑care access for vulnerable populations (elderly, low‑income, LGBTQ, immigrant).  
* Explain how geography (proximity to the Bay, dispersed towns) shapes risk and response.  
* Provide the data‑driven context that policymakers, advocates, and the public need to make informed decisions.

---

## 2. Recurring Themes in Recent Coverage  

| Theme | Why It’s Significant | Key Institutions & Stakeholders | Typical Situations & Illustrative Stories |
|-------|----------------------|--------------------------------|-------------------------------------------|
| **A. Crime & Gun Violence** | Direct threat to life; influences public‑health (trauma, mental‑health) and community trust in law enforcement. | • Caroline County Police Accountability Board (PCAB) – **John Bartlett**, **Kimberly Raeder**  <br>• County Sheriff’s Offices (Caroline, Talbot) <br>• Maryland State Police <br>• Local courts (juvenile & adult) | • *“Police Accountability Board reports progress”* – Feb 14 2024 (PCAB annual report; only 6 complaints from >26,000 calls)【https://stardem.com/news/19738B6970702968】 <br>• *“Police investigating shooting in Greensboro”* – Feb 12 2025 (multiple shooters, no injuries yet)【https://stardem.com/news/19EB92016F693118】 <br>• *“16‑year‑old charged with attempted murder in Federalsburg”* – May 22 2025 (juvenile charged as adult)【https://stardem.com/news/1A0C005AD16EB138】 |
| **B. Human Trafficking & Exploitation** | Hidden crime with severe health‑related consequences (physical, mental, reproductive). | • For All Seasons (non‑profit) – **Lauren Weber**, **Katie Theeke** <br>• Talbot County Council (council members **Keasha Haythe**, **Pete Lesher**) <br>• Maryland Attorney General’s Office | • *“For All Seasons gives update on human trafficking in Talbot County”* – Jan 31 2025 (ongoing cases, increased awareness)【https://stardem.com/news/19E732FE924F9770】 |
| **C. Health‑Care Planning & Aging** | Maryland’s senior population is growing; advanced‑directive knowledge reduces emergency‑room strain and improves end‑of‑life care. | • Talbot Hospice <br>• Local hospitals (Johns Hopkins Baltimore, University of Maryland Medical Center) <br>• Maryland Department of Health | • *“Advanced Healthcare Planning”* – Feb 23 2024 (free workshops on advance directives)【https://stardem.com/news/19D413D1ACF5DB70】 |
| **D. Mental‑Health & Cognitive Wellness** | Dementia and memory‑loss affect a large share of rural seniors; community resources are limited. | • Shore Neurocognitive & Behavioral Health <br>• Local senior centers <br>• Maryland Office of Aging | • *“Memory loss and dementia talk”* – Feb 23 2024 (free public education session)【https://stardem.com/news/1976225C0A0B59A0】 |
| **E. Regulatory & Infrastructure Health Impacts** | Water‑quality, sewer fees, and flood mitigation directly affect public‑health outcomes (e.g., water‑borne illness). | • Trappe Town Council (water‑sewer ordinance) <br>• Talbot County Liquor Board (license & alcohol‑related health issues) <br>• Maryland Department of the Environment | • *“Trappe Council introduces ordinance to raise water and sewer usage fees”* – May 3 2024【https://stardem.com/news/198D357DE7E9A6C0】 <br>• *“Talbot County Liquor Board license applications available”* – Jan 10 2025 (alcohol regulation & health)【https://stardem.com/news/19E09ACFD858B638】 |
| **F. Emerging Cannabis Regulation** | New market creates economic opportunity but raises concerns about impaired driving and youth exposure. | • Easton Town Council (cannabis zoning ordinance) <br>• Maryland Department of Health (public‑health monitoring) <br>• Maryland Department of Commerce | • *“Easton Town Council passes cannabis ordinance”* – Apr 17 2024 (zoning for dispensaries, no on‑site consumption)【https://stardem.com/news/1987EE1E05846A20】 |
| **G. School‑Related Safety & Inclusion** | Policies such as “Safe Space” stickers affect student mental‑health and school climate. | • Calvert County Board of Education (policy decision) <br>• School administrators <br>• Advocacy groups (Beau Biden Foundation, LGBTQ organizations) | • *“Removal of Safe Space stickers draws criticism”* – May 15 2025 (board vote, community backlash)【https://stardem.com/news/1A0A23BEEF50B390】 |

> **Note:** Several of these stories are still developing (e.g., the Federalsburg shooting investigation, the implementation of PCAB recommendations, and the long‑term effects of the cannabis ordinance). Verify current status before publishing.

*Tip:* When you meet a person for the first time, ask about their **policy goals**, **recent actions**, and **data they rely on**. Keep a running “contact sheet” (name, title, email, beat, recent quotes) to reference later.

---

## 4. Core Institutions & How They Interact with Health & Safety  

| Institution | Primary Functions | Typical Interaction with Health/Public‑Safety Topics |
|-------------|-------------------|------------------------------------------------------|
| **Caroline County Police Accountability Board (PCAB)** | Receives citizen complaints, recommends policy, and can refer cases for disciplinary action. | Reviews use‑of‑force incidents, monitors body‑camera activation, issues public reports (e.g., 2023 report). |
| **Talbot County Liquor Board** | Issues liquor licenses, enforces state alcohol statutes. | Regulates alcohol availability, which impacts community health (e.g., under‑age drinking, DUI rates). |
| **Easton Town Council** | Governs zoning, public‑works, and local ordinances. | Passed cannabis zoning ordinance; decisions affect public‑health monitoring of cannabis‑related incidents. |
| **For All Seasons (Non‑profit)** | Provides victim services, training, and advocacy on human trafficking. | Offers community workshops, collaborates with law‑enforcement, reports trends to council. |
| **Talbot County Council of Arts & Caroline County Council of Arts** | Sponsors cultural events, sometimes fund health‑related awareness (e.g., Human‑Trafficking exhibits). | Hosts exhibitions that double as public‑education on health‑risk topics. |
| **Maryland Department of Health (MDH)** | Sets statewide health policies, monitors disease trends, administers vaccination programs. | Provides data for reporting on opioid overdose rates, pandemic impacts, and mental‑health resources. |
| **Maryland Department of the Environment (MDE)** | Regulates water‑quality, sewage, and flood‑plain management. | Oversees water‑sewer fee hikes, flood mitigation projects that affect public‑health (e.g., water‑borne illness). |
| **Local Hospitals & Health Systems** (Johns Hopkins, UM Medical Center) | Provide acute care, specialty services, and community health outreach. | Source for statistics on trauma, opioid overdoses, and aging‑population health outcomes. |
| **School Boards & Superintendents** (Caroline County Public Schools) | Set education policy, student safety protocols. | Debate virtual instruction, Safe‑Space stickers, and mental‑health programming. |
| **Sheriff’s Offices (Caroline, Talbot)** | Law‑enforcement, jail operations, community policing. | Respond to shootings, manage investigations, cooperate with PCAB. |
| **Community Organizations** (e.g., American Legion, Senior Centers, Faith Groups) | Offer social services, food security, and emergency‑response volunteers. | Partner in disaster relief (floods), provide venues for health‑education events. |

**Interaction Patterns**  
1. **Data Sharing:** Police boards request incident reports from sheriff’s offices; health departments request overdose data from hospitals.  
2. **Policy Feedback Loops:** Community groups (e.g., For All Seasons) submit recommendations to county councils; councils vote on ordinances (cannabis, water fees).  
3. **Funding & Grants:** State agencies (MDE, MDH) provide grants that enable local health‑planning workshops (e.g., advanced‑directive sessions).  

---

## 5. Geographic Context – How Place Shapes Health & Safety  

| Geographic Unit | Key Characteristics | Health/Public‑Safety Implications |
|----------------|---------------------|-----------------------------------|
| **Talbot County (Easton, St. Michael’s, Oxford)** | Rural, affluent pockets, high proportion of seniors, extensive shoreline. | Aging‑related health services needed; flood risk from Bay raises water‑borne disease concerns; tourism creates seasonal spikes in DUI & drug incidents. |
| **Caroline County (Federalsburg, Denton, Greensboro)** | Mixed‑economy (agriculture, manufacturing), higher poverty rates, limited specialty care. | Higher opioid use, limited access to trauma centers, greater vulnerability to human‑trafficking pipelines. |
| **Dorchester County (Cambridge, St. Michaels)** | Large African‑American community, historic reliance on fishing/shipbuilding. | Elevated rates of chronic disease (cardiovascular), occupational hazards, need for culturally‑competent health outreach. |
| **Queen Anne’s County (Centreville, Stevensville)** | Faster‑growing suburbia, commuter population to Baltimore/Washington. | Increased traffic‑related injuries, pressure on mental‑health services for commuters. |
| **Kent County (Chestertown, Worton)** | Small towns, significant college student population (Washington College). | Youth‑focused health issues (STI, substance use) and need for campus‑community coordination. |
| **Western Shore (Baltimore, Howard, Montgomery, Prince George’s)** | Urban/suburban contrast; often the “reference” for state‑wide health metrics. | Provides comparative data for rural‑urban health disparity studies. |

**Why It Matters**  
* **Distance to Care:** Residents may travel 45–90 minutes for tertiary care, affecting outcomes for stroke, trauma, and mental‑health crises.  
* **Environmental Hazards:** Low‑lying towns (e.g., Federalsburg) experience frequent flooding; standing water can foster mosquito‑borne illness and disrupt sewage.  
* **Demographic Diversity:** Growing Hispanic populations (see Hispanic Heritage month story, Oct 10 2023) create language‑access needs for public‑health messaging.  
* **Economic Drivers:** Tourism in St. Michael’s and Oxford raises seasonal demand for emergency services and influences local substance‑abuse patterns.

---

## 6. Essential Sources & Data Sets  

| Source | What to Track | How to Access / Build Relationship |
|--------|---------------|------------------------------------|
| **County Police Departments & Sheriff’s Offices** | Crime logs, incident reports, body‑camera footage requests, use‑of‑force data. | Attend monthly police‑department briefings; request FOIA logs; maintain contact with Public‑Information Officers (e.g., *John Bartlett* for PCAB). |
| **Maryland State Police – Crime Statistics** | County‑level crime trends, firearm incidents. | Download quarterly reports from **MDPS** website; subscribe to the “MD Crime Data” email list. |
| **Maryland Department of Health** | Opioid‑related deaths, overdose EMS calls, infectious‑disease outbreaks, senior health indicators. | Use the **MDH Data Portal**; sign up for health‑alert newsletters; request de‑identified hospital discharge data. |
| **County Health Departments (Talbot, Caroline, Dorchester)** | Water‑quality reports, sewage treatment compliance, flood‑plain maps. | Attend county health board meetings; request “public health dashboards.” |
| **Hospitals & Health‑Systems** (Johns Hopkins, UM Medical Center) | Emergency‑room volume, trauma‑center statistics, mental‑health admissions. | Contact hospital public‑relations offices; request annual community‑health reports. |
| **School Boards & Superintendents** | Discipline data, bullying reports, mental‑health program budgets, virtual‑learning policies. | Join school‑board meeting webinars; request minutes; develop rapport with **Andraé Townsel** and district counselors. |
| **Non‑profits (For All Seasons, American Legion, Senior Centers)** | Service utilization numbers, victim‑support statistics, community‑event attendance. | Attend quarterly board meetings; volunteer for events to meet staff. |
| **State Legislative Tracker (Maryland General Assembly)** | Bills affecting health (e.g., cannabis, water‑sewer fees, human‑trafficking statutes). | Follow the **Legislative Information System (LIS)**; set alerts for keywords. |
| **Court Dockets (Maryland Judiciary)** | Status of criminal cases (e.g., Federalsburg shooting, teen attempted‑murder case). | Use **MD Courts’ online docket search**; monitor case numbers (e.g., *2025‑CR‑0223*). |
| **Local Media Archives** (Star Democrat, The Cavalier) | Historical context, prior coverage, quotes. | Create a searchable PDF library; note story IDs (e.g., *news/19EB92016F693118*). |

**Daily/Weekly Habits**

1. **Morning Scan** – Check MDH alerts, police logs, and county board meeting calendars.  
2. **Mid‑day Check‑In** – Send quick emails to primary contacts (e.g., PCAB chair, For All Seasons VP) confirming any new releases.  
3. **Evening Review** – Scan local social‑media groups (Easton Community Forum, Facebook “Talbot County Residents”) for emerging concerns.  
4. **Document Everything** – Keep a spreadsheet of sources, dates of contact, and what was promised (e.g., “receive body‑cam footage by 5 pm 4/30”).  
5. **FOIA Calendar** – Mark filing deadlines for public‑record requests (e.g., police‑department incident logs due within 15 days of request).  

---

## 7. Story‑Idea Bank – Under‑Covered Angles & Emerging Issues  

Below are ten story ideas, each with a suggested angle, potential sources, and a note on why the story matters now.

| # | Working Title & Angle | Why It’s Newsworthy | Primary Sources / Data |
|---|-----------------------|---------------------|------------------------|
| 1 | **“After the Shot: Tracking the Federalsburg Teen Murder‑Attempt Case”** – Follow‑up on legal outcomes, victim support, and community response. | The case is still pending; outcomes will affect local perception of juvenile‑as‑adult prosecution. | Court docket (2025‑CR‑0223), Sheriff’s Office press releases, interviews with victim‑advocacy groups. |
| 2 | **“Do Police‑Accountability Recommendations Actually Change the Beat?”** – Examine implementation of PCAB’s 2023 recommendations (body‑camera policy, training). | Accountability reforms are a national focus; local impact is measurable via complaint trends. | PCAB reports, interviews with **John Bartlett**, data from Maryland State Police on body‑camera activation rates. |
| 3 | **“Human‑Trafficking Detection: Has For All Seasons’ Training Cut the Numbers?”** – Analyze pre‑ and post‑training incident reports in Talbot County. | Early data suggest increased reporting; need to confirm if this reflects real trend or awareness effect. | For All Seasons program stats, police trafficking unit logs, councilmember **Keasha Haythe** statements. |
| 4 | **“Cannabis Dispensaries & Road Safety: A Six‑Month Review”** – Correlate traffic‑stop and DUI data with new dispensaries opened after April 2024 ordinance. | New market could affect impaired‑driving rates; a public‑health metric that matters to residents. | Maryland Dept. of Transportation crash data, Maryland Motor Vehicle Administration DUI arrests, interviews with **Andy Harris** and local EMS. |
| 5 | **“Rising Water‑Sewer Fees: Who Pays the Most?”** – Investigate impact of Trappe’s fee hike on low‑income households; link to health outcomes (e.g., water‑borne disease). | Fee increases can strain vulnerable families and affect sanitation. | Trappe Town Council meeting minutes, utility bill samples, interviews with **Mayor Brian Schmidt**, Maryland Dept. of the Environment. |
| 6 | **“Safe Space or Safe Place? The Aftermath of Removing LGBTQ Stickers in Calvert Schools”** – Examine student mental‑health metrics and parental feedback. | Policy reversal sparked community debate; mental‑health consequences are under‑reported. | Calvert County Board of Education minutes, interviews with students, counseling staff, LGBTQ advocacy groups. |
| 7 | **“Advanced Directive Workshops: Are Seniors Signing Up?”** – Measure attendance at Talbot Hospice’s advanced‑healthcare planning events and subsequent adoption rates. | Early planning reduces emergency‑room strain; community uptake unknown. | Talbot Hospice event registries, interviews with **Victoria Bradley**, Medicare data on advance‑directive filings. |
| 8 | **“Flood Preparedness After the Federalsburg Exhibit”** – Evaluate whether the new museum exhibit has spurred concrete mitigation projects (e.g., levee upgrades). | Flood risk is a chronic health hazard; public awareness must translate into action. | Caroline County Emergency Management, interviews with **Mayor Kim Abner** of Federalsburg, FEMA grant data. |
| 9 | **“Mental‑Health Gaps for Rural Seniors: From Dementia Talks to Real Services”** – Map existing services vs. demand in Talbot & Caroline counties. | Growing elderly population; limited specialized care. | Shore Neurocognitive & Behavioral Health attendance logs, Maryland Aging Services data, interviews with senior‑center directors. |
|10| **“Alcohol Licensing & Community Health: Does the Talbot Liquor Board’s Process Reduce DUI Rates?”** – Compare DUI statistics before and after the 2025‑2026 licensing cycle. | Licensing can affect outlet density; ties to public‑health outcomes. | Liquor Board application data, Maryland State Police DUI statistics, interviews with **Victoria Bradley** and local bar owners. |

**Tip:** Before pitching, verify whether any of these angles have already been covered in the past six months. Use the story‑ID list in your archive to avoid duplication.

---

## 8. Putting It All Together – A Typical Reporting Workflow  

1. **Identify a Beat Trigger** – e.g., a new PCAB report released, a council meeting agenda item, or a spike in EMS calls.  
2. **Gather Primary Data** – Pull the official document, request supplemental data (FOIA if needed), and note any gaps.  
3. **Reach Out to Sources** – Contact the institution’s spokesperson within 24 hours; schedule an interview before the deadline.  
4. **Contextualize** – Use historical data (e.g., 2023 vs. 2025 crime stats) and geographic factors (rural vs. suburban).  
5. **Verify Status** – Check if any pending lawsuits or policy changes could alter the story (e.g., pending protest against a liquor license).  
6. **Draft with Transparency** – Quote sources accurately, attribute data, and include hyperlinks to original reports.  
7. **Fact‑Check & Legal Review** – Especially for criminal cases or pending legislation.  
8. **Publish & Follow‑Up** – Schedule a “check‑in” story 3–6 months later to report on outcomes (e.g., implementation of PCAB recommendations).  

---

## 9. Quick Reference Cheat‑Sheet (Bullet Lists Only)

### Sources to Prioritize  

- **Police & Sheriff’s Offices** – Public‑Info Officers (e.g., John Bartlett).  
- **County Health Departments** – Water‑quality & disease surveillance.  
- **Hospital PR Offices** – Emergency‑room data, overdose trends.  
- **Non‑profits** – For All Seasons, American Legion, senior centers.  
- **State Agencies** – MDH, MDE, Maryland Department of Public Safety & Correctional Services.  

### Data Sets to Bookmark  

- MD Police‑Crime Statistics Dashboard.  
- Maryland Department of Health “Opioid Overdose” tracker.  
- County Water‑Sewer Fee schedules (Trappe, Talbot).  
- Court docket search (MD Courts).  
- Legislative bill tracker (e.g., House Bill 805 on cannabis).  

### Daily Habits  

- **Morning:** Scan police logs & health alerts.  
- **Midday:** Email primary contacts, log any promised data.  
- **Evening:** Update story tracker, note upcoming council meetings.  

---

### Final Thought  

Health and public safety on the Eastern Shore are intertwined with geography, economics, and community culture. By grounding every story in **hard data**, **institutional context**, and **human impact**, you’ll produce reporting that informs, holds power accountable, and ultimately improves the lives of Easton’s residents. Good luck, and keep the public’s right to know at the center of your work!

## Option 3:

- Used this command, provided by copilot to filter and find a random sample of 100 stories between local government, health, and public safety:

```bash 
uv run sqlite-utils query stardem_entities.db   "SELECT * FROM stories ORDER BY RANDOM() LIMIT 100"   --json-cols > filtered_stories.json
```

I am using filtered_stories.json. 

# Copilot Convo:
lcharle1: How do I edit this line of command to filter by a certain quantity: uv run sqlite-utils memory stories.db \
  "SELECT * FROM stories WHERE content LIKE '%Cambridge%'" \
  --json-cols > filtered_stories.json

GitHub Copilot: To filter by a certain quantity (limit the number of results), add a `LIMIT` clause to the SQL query:

```bash
uv run sqlite-utils memory stories.db \
  "SELECT * FROM stories WHERE content LIKE '%Cambridge%' LIMIT 100" \
  --json-cols > filtered_stories.json
```

Replace `100` with whatever number of stories you want. This will return only the first 100 stories matching your filter criteria.

If you want more control over which stories you get, you can also add `ORDER BY`:

```bash
uv run sqlite-utils memory stories.db \
  "SELECT * FROM stories WHERE content LIKE '%Cambridge%' ORDER BY date DESC LIMIT 100" \
  --json-cols > filtered_stories.json
```

This would give you the 100 most recent Cambridge stories.

lcharle1: make it a random sample

GitHub Copilot: To get a random sample, use `ORDER BY RANDOM()` with `LIMIT`:

```bash
uv run sqlite-utils memory stories.db \
  "SELECT * FROM stories WHERE content LIKE '%Cambridge%' ORDER BY RANDOM() LIMIT 100" \
  --json-cols > filtered_stories.json
```

This will give you a random sample of 100 stories that mention Cambridge. Each time you run it, you'll get a different random selection.

lcharle1: UnicodeDecodeError: 'utf-8' codec can't decode byte 0xab in position 31: invalid start byte

GitHub Copilot: The error indicates that `stories.db` has encoding issues. Try using the correct command - you need to read from the database file with the `--load-extension` flag or use the correct table name. Let me check what's in your directory: 

Ran terminal command: ls -lh

Ran terminal command: cd /workspaces/jour329w_fall2025/charleston/stardem_draft && ls -lh

The issue is that `sqlite-utils memory` is trying to read `stories.db` as if it's a JSON file. Since you already have the database created, use this command instead: 

Ran terminal command: uv run sqlite-utils query stories.db "SELECT * FROM stories WHERE content LIKE '%Cambridge%' ORDER BY RANDOM() LIMIT 100" --json-cols > filtered_stories.json

Use `sqlite-utils query` instead of `sqlite-utils memory` when working with an existing database file:

```bash
uv run sqlite-utils query stories.db \
  "SELECT * FROM stories WHERE content LIKE '%Cambridge%' ORDER BY RANDOM() LIMIT 100" \
  --json-cols > filtered_stories.json
```

The command has run successfully and created your filtered file with 100 random Cambridge stories.

Use the attached filtered_stories.json dataset of stories as your primary source material. 

Include interactive elements such as: micro-learning (bite-sized lessons) and gamification (streaks, points). Use inspiration from other interactive learning apps such as Quizlet or Duolingo. 
"


- model: groq/openai/gpt-oss-120b

It does miss the visual aspect. I will emphasize a visual aspect in the next trial. Since the filter_stories.json does not include any images I can see this not working but maybe it can create images.

## Trial 5 - Learning App with Copilot

Using Copilot to create the learning app. 

- Eval: Unsuccessful. It created a one question quiz lesson. Smaller than expected. More clarity, in instructions and examples, is needed to execute this format better. The only location mentioned was Easton. The other sections were titled 'Said', 'County', 'Info', 'All', and 'Center', but it all included the same information. The same 'Why it matters explanation for three stories. 

I am happy it is possible though. This gives hope for different formats to be created, visual ones too, with the help of copilot. 

## Evaluation

Overall, between the learning app and pitch deck format, what dictates a good Beat Book is what provides the most of what you are looking for. If it is detail and narrative, a training guide is best because it can give you the most information. If it is a quick debrief, then the pitch deck format is great. 

It was difficult to experiment with the learning app style because it delivered the results in a how to guide to create the app, rather than giving me the information from filtered_stories.json as a learning app. This is probably because the LLM is not familiar with creating apps so it did not know how to present the information that way, but could give me a guide in creating one.

Switching to copilot to try to create the app was a good idea but the output was unsuccessful. Definitely needs more guidance in what is expected. A different format than the way we've been writing prompts. Maybe step by step?

I was content with the pitch deck's delivery of the information but it lacked detail. If I was a seasoned reporter coming to Talbot for the first time, I think I could do well with the pitch deck style, but a newly graduated reporter moving to Talbot, the training guide would suite them better. 
=== stardem_nearly_final_prototype_v1.md ===
**Talbot County – Health & Public‑Safety Beat  
A Learning‑App Style Training Manual (2023‑2025 Stories)**  

---

## 1.  How to use this “app”  

| Feature | What it does | How you get points |
|---------|--------------|-------------------|
| **Micro‑Learning Bites** | 2‑minute “flashcards” that explain a term, law, or local practice (e.g., “What is the Maryland Police Accountability Act 2021?”) | +10 pts per bite completed; streak bonus +5 pts each day you finish a bite |
| **Story‑Quiz Challenges** | After reading a story, answer 3‑question multiple‑choice or true/false mini‑quiz. | +20 pts per perfect quiz; +5 pts for each attempt |
| **Badge Levels** | Collect badges for mastering a theme (e.g., **“Water‑Fee Warrior”** after finishing the water‑rate module) | Badges unlock “quick‑reference cards” you can pull up while reporting |
| **Streaks** | Keep a daily streak of any activity (reading, quizzing, posting a note). | Every 5‑day streak = +30 pts; 30‑day streak = **Gold Reporter** badge |
| **Leaderboard** | See where you rank among the cohort of early‑career reporters. | Friendly competition – the top 3 each month earn a coffee voucher (real‑world reward). |

> **Tip:** Treat each story as a “mission”. Open the story, complete the micro‑learning bite that explains its background, then take the quiz. You’ll finish a “mission” and earn points, plus you’ll already have the context you need for the next beat.

---

## 2.  Step‑by‑Step Narrative: Getting on the Beat  

1. **Orientation – Know the Terrain**  
   *Open the “Map” section of the app.*  You’ll see the five municipalities (Easton, Oxford, Queen Anne’s, St. Michael’s, Trappe) and the key waterways (Chesapeake Bay, Miles River, Eastern Bay, Choptank River, Marshy Hope Creek). Click any dot to pull up a short geography flashcard (2 min).  

2. **Core Themes – Build Your Knowledge Base**  
   *Select “Health & Safety Themes”* – you’ll see six modules (Crime & Policing, Substance‑Use & Treatment, Environmental‑Health, Regulatory Policy, Human‑Trafficking, Community Well‑Being). Each module contains:  
   * a 2‑min micro‑learning video,  
   * a “Key Stats” card,  
   * a short “Story Bank” of the most relevant articles (see Section 3).  

3. **Daily Mission** – Pick one story from the Story Bank, read it, and do the associated quiz.  

4. **Apply the Knowledge** – Draft a quick “beat‑note” (150‑word summary) that answers:  
   * Who, what, when, where, why?  
   * What are the angles?  
   * Who do I need to talk to next?  

5. **Earn Points → Unlock Interviews** – After you collect 200 pts you unlock a **“Contact‑Card”** that contains email/phone numbers for a key institution (e.g., Talbot County Liquor Board). Use it to set up a real interview – the app logs the outcome as extra points.  

6. **Reflect & Pitch** – At the end of each week, the app prompts you to write a 2‑sentence pitch for a possible story (future‑focus, under‑reported angle). The AI‑coach gives quick feedback; a polished pitch earns a **“Pitch‑Ready”** badge.

---

## 3.  Recurring Themes & Issues (derived from the 2023‑2025 filtered_stories.json)

| Theme | Why it matters to the Health & Public‑Safety beat | Representative Stories (date, title) |
|-------|---------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------|
| **Police & Accountability** | Shows how law‑enforcement interacts with community health (trauma, trust). | *“Caroline Police Accountability Board reports progress”* (02‑14‑2024), *“Police investigating shooting in Greensboro”* (02‑12‑2025) – the latter is a neighboring‑county case that often influences Talbot policy. |
| **Substance‑Use & Treatment** | Opioid, alcohol, and teen vaping impact emergency services and public‑health funding. | *“Harris speaks to Dorchester Chamber after rally calling for town hall”* (03‑14‑2025 – mentions mass firings, indirect health‑job impact). |
| **Environmental & Infrastructure Health** | Water‑rate hikes, flood‑history exhibits, bridge projects affect community wellness and equity. | *“Trappe Council introduces ordinance to raise water and sewer usage fees”* (05‑03‑2024), *“Cost for pedestrian trail bridge connector estimated at 8 million”* (12‑05‑2024). |
| **Human‑Trafficking & Exploitation** | Direct public‑safety concern; also links to mental‑health services. | *“For All Seasons gives update on human trafficking in Talbot County”* (01‑31‑2025). |
| **Regulatory Changes (Cannabis, Liquor)** | New markets create health‑policy gaps (e.g., impaired‑driving, under‑age use). | *“Easton Town Council passes cannabis ordinance”* (04‑17‑2024), *“Talbot County Liquor Board license applications available”* (01‑10‑2025). |
| **Community‑Based Health Programs** | Senior centers, yoga, free clinics, food‑pantries, mental‑health outreach – all part of the safety net. | *“Yoga class – Oxford Community Center”* (multiple listings), *“Advanced Healthcare Planning – Talbot Hospice”* (02‑14‑2024). |
| **Education & Youth Safety** | School‑based programs (anti‑trafficking courses, health‑planning workshops) shape future health outcomes. | *“For All Seasons – 120 people took free anti‑human‑trafficking course”* (01‑31‑2025). |

> **Pattern:** Most stories combine **policy** (legislation, board decisions) with **human impact** (citizens’ health, safety, or economic wellbeing). That intersection is your reporting sweet‑spot.

> **Pro tip:** Keep a “Contact Card” for each institution in your notebook (or in the app). Add a line for “best time to call” and “recent policy change” – it’ll save you minutes on the ground.

> **Quick‑look:** When you need a source, start with the person who **authored** the story (e.g., Lauren Weber for trafficking). Then expand outward to the institution’s spokesperson.

| **“Bridge to Health” – Cost‑benefit of the MD 50 pedestrian bridge for cyclists & walkers** | Quantify expected reduction in traffic‑injury ER visits vs. $8 M expense. | MDOT, Talbot County Planning Dept., local EMS data. |
| **“License to Serve” – Community reaction to Talbot Liquor Board’s automatic renewal rule** | Investigate whether the lack of public hearings masks problems (e.g., DUI spikes). | Liquor Board Secretary Victoria Bradley, local bar owners, police DUI stats. |
| **“Trafficking Trends” – Follow‑up on For All Seasons’ 2025 training numbers** | Profile a survivor who used the program; examine law‑enforcement partnership. | Lauren Weber, local sheriff’s office, survivor (with consent). |
| **“Cannabis & the Kids” – Early‑intervention in schools after dispensary openings** | Look at whether school counseling services are prepared for increased exposure. | Talbot County Public Schools Superintendent Derek Simmons, health‑teacher focus groups. |
| **“Senior Isolation” – Evaluating the effect of yoga & needle‑group classes on fall rates** | Use pre‑/post‑attendance health records (with IRB clearance). | Brookletts Place Senior Center staff, local PTs, participants. |
| **“Emergency‑Weather Virtual School” – Talbot’s contingency plan** | Compare with Caroline County’s rejected virtual‑day plan; interview parents, teachers. | Talbot County Board of Education, teachers’ union, parent‑teacher association. |
| **“Off‑shore Wind & Local Health” – Noise & shadow‑flicker concerns for nearby residents** | Collect health‑complaint logs from Easton and St. Michael’s residents. | US Wind public‑relations, Maryland Dept. of the Environment, local doctors. |
| **“Police‑Community Trust” – Mapping complaint rates across Talbot’s municipalities** | Visual data story: why some towns report more complaints than others. | Police Accountability Board (if Talbot adopts similar system), city council records. |
| **“Youth Sports & Injury Prevention” – After the recent field‑hockey showdown, look at concussion protocols** | Follow up on the “Sabres ride Webb’s goal” story; interview athletic trainers. | Easton High School athletic director, Talbot County health‑clinic. |

> **Tip:** For each pitch, write a one‑sentence “why now?” line that ties the story to a current policy discussion or upcoming event (e.g., a council meeting, a community health fair).
