## 10.  Caveats & Unresolved Storylines  

| Issue | Current status (as of latest story) | Caveat for reporting |
|-------|--------------------------------------|----------------------|
| **Cannabis Ordinance (Easton)** | Ordinance passed (04‑17‑2024) but **implementation details** (e.g., permitting, enforcement) still pending Governor’s sign‑off. | Verify the latest status before filing “new‑business” angle. |
| **Water‑rate hike (Trappe)** | Ordinance introduced; **final vote** scheduled for the next council meeting (date TBD). | Keep an eye on council minutes; the final rate may differ. |
| **Pedestrian bridge cost** | $8.1 M estimate; **final location** not yet selected. | Any claim about exact route or construction timeline is premature. |
| **Human‑trafficking statistics** | For All Seasons reports “still happening” but **exact case numbers** for 2025 not released. | Use “reported” figures with attribution; avoid stating definitive prevalence. |
| **Virtual‑learning policy (Caroline County)** – not Talbot but influences state‑wide discussion. | Board concluded virtual days **not feasible** (04‑08‑2025). | If quoting, note it applies to Caroline County; Talbot may decide differently. |
| **Police complaints** – Caroline data used as proxy. | Talbot has not released a comparable report. | Treat Caroline figures as illustration, not Talbot baseline. |
| **Offshore wind project timeline** | Energy delivery slated for **Dec 2028** (Sep 2024 article); still **subject to permitting**. | Do not claim the wind farm will be operational before the stated date. |
| **Human‑trafficking training numbers** – “more than 120” participants. | No breakdown by age, gender, or outcomes. | If you need demographics, request them directly from the nonprofit. |

> **Best practice:** Always note the date of the source in your story lead (“as of Jan 31 2025…”). That signals to editors that you are aware of possible changes.

| **Human‑Trafficking Tracker** | Interviewing survivors or NGOs. | Key program stats, contact list (Weber, Theeke), recent legislation. |
| **Police‑Accountability Primer** | Reporting on complaints or board actions. | Flowchart of complaint → Administrative Charging Committee → Trial Board. |
| **Senior‑Wellness Snapshot** | Story on yoga, needle groups, health‑planning. | Attendance numbers, program costs, health‑outcome indicators (falls reduction). |

You unlock a card by earning **150 pts** in the related module. Keep them on your phone for fast copy‑and‑paste of verified facts.

## 12.  Putting It All Together – A Mini‑Workflow Example  

1. **Morning** – Open the **Map** → tap **St. Michael’s** → read the *Geography* flashcard (2 min).  
2. **Select Module** – “Environmental‑Health.” Open the **Pedestrian Bridge** micro‑learning (2 min) and quiz (5 pts).  
3. **Story Mission** – Read *“Cost for pedestrian trail bridge connector estimated at 8 million”* (12‑05‑2024). Answer the 3‑question quiz (+20 pts).  
4. **Beat‑Note** – Draft a 150‑word note: “MD 50 bridge could cut cyclist injuries by X %; Talbot residents may see a 10‑minute walk‑time reduction…”  
5. **Contact Card** – Unlock **“Bridge to Health”** badge → get MDOT contact email → send interview request.  
6. **Pitch** – At day’s end, write a two‑sentence pitch: “Will the $8 M MD 50 bridge lower Talbot’s traffic‑injury ER visits? I’ll compare projected injury‑rate data with similar projects in Maryland.” Submit to editor; earn **Pitch‑Ready** badge.

## 13.  Final Thought  

The health‑and‑public‑safety beat in Talbot County lives at the intersection of **policy, community‑level services, and individual stories**. By treating each story as a mission, using the micro‑learning nuggets to build context, and constantly looping back to the **who, what, where, why** of every issue, you’ll quickly move from “new reporter” to **trusted local beat specialist**.  

Good luck, and may your streaks be long and your leads plentiful!

=== stardem_nearly_final_prototype_v13.md ===
# Health & Public Safety Beat – Talbot County & Municipalities  
*(Easton, Oxford, Queen Anne’s, St. Michaels, Trappe)*  

**Purpose** – This guide is meant for an early‑career reporter who already knows the basics of journalism but has never been on the ground in Talbot County.  It gives you the geography, people, institutions, and recurring issues that shape health‑and‑public‑safety stories in the five municipalities.  You’ll learn where to start each story, who to talk to, and how the county’s past has shaped its present and future.  

---  

## 1.  Setting the Scene  

Talbot County, Maryland, sits on the Chesapeake Bay and is bordered by the Atlantic Ocean to the south and the Choptank River to the north.  The five towns that make up the county are **Easton** (the county seat), **Oxford**, **Queen Anne’s**, **St. Michaels**, and **Trappe**.  Each has its own character, but all share a few common threads that influence public‑safety and health reporting:  

| Municipality | Population (est.) | Key Economic Drivers | Health & Safety Notes |
|--------------|-------------------|----------------------|-----------------------|
| Easton | ~13 000 | Government, logistics, small‑business hubs | Frequent public‑safety meetings (town council, police). |
| Oxford | ~4 200 | Agriculture, tourism, small‑scale manufacturing | Focus on rural health services and emergency response. |
| Queen Anne’s | ~8 000 | Education (high schools), agriculture | Rising concerns over mental‑health resources for teens. |
| St. Michaels | ~4 200 | Tourism, maritime industries | Coastal flooding and storm‑related health risks. |
| Trappe | ~2 700 | Agriculture, small‑scale industry | Limited water‑and‑sewer infrastructure, recent fee hikes. |

**Why focus on the future?**  Recent policy changes and emerging challenges—such as the 2024 cannabis ordinance, the 2024 water‑fee increase, and the ongoing human‑trafficking awareness effort—signal a shift toward more proactive health and safety governance.  Understanding these developments will help you anticipate what stories are coming next, rather than simply rehashing past incidents.

---

| **For All Seasons** | Non‑profit anti‑human‑trafficking network | *For All Seasons gives update on human trafficking* (Jan 31 2025) | [Link](https://www.stardem.com/news/19E732FE924F9770) |

These entities are your primary sources.  When you need data, policy language, or a statement, start with the relevant board or council minutes, public‑record documents, or official statements.

## 3.  Themes that Re‑appear  

Below are the recurring issues that emerge from the dataset.  For each, I describe why it matters, who the stakeholders are, the competing viewpoints, and the kinds of situations you’ll see in the field.  I also point to a story that illustrates the theme, with dates and links.

| Theme | Why It Matters | Key Stakeholders | Competing Viewpoints | Typical Story Situations | Example Story |
|-------|----------------|------------------|----------------------|--------------------------|---------------|
| **1. Police Accountability & Oversight** | Public trust hinges on transparent, accountable law‑enforcement practices. | Police departments, Police Accountability Board, local residents, advocacy groups. | **Supporters** say oversight deters misconduct; **Critics** argue it hampers officers and stifles community policing. | Complaints, investigations, body‑camera usage, disciplinary outcomes. | *Caroline Police Accountability Board reports progress* (Feb 14 2024) – 6 complaints, 1 minor infraction, body‑camera gaps noted. |
| **2. Public‑Safety Infrastructure & Funding** | Aging water/sewer systems pose health risks; funding decisions affect community resilience. | Town councils, residents, businesses, state agencies. | **Pro‑fee‑increase** argue for necessary upgrades; **Opponents** worry about tax burdens and utility cost spikes. | Infrastructure audits, fee hikes, grant applications, public hearings. | *Trappe Council introduces ordinance to raise water & sewer usage fees* (May 3 2024) – $6.20 per 1,000 gallons for water, $11.48 per 1,000 gallons for sewer. |
| **3. Substance‑Use Policy (Cannabis)** | Legalization can alter public‑health outcomes, tax revenue, and law‑enforcement resource allocation. | Town councils, cannabis businesses, public‑health agencies, residents. | **Pro‑legalization** highlight economic benefits; **Opponents** point to potential health risks and traffic accidents. | Ordinance debates, licensing, zoning, enforcement guidelines. | *Easton Town Council passes cannabis ordinance* (Apr 17 2024) – permits dispensaries in central and commercial districts, prohibits on‑premise consumption. |
| **4. Human Trafficking Awareness** | Trafficking is a hidden public‑health issue that can lead to abuse, disease spread, and mental‑health trauma. | For All Seasons, law‑enforcement, victims, community organizations. | **Advocacy groups** push for stronger detection and support; **Others** question resource allocation. | Training courses, hotline calls, victim interviews, policy updates. | *For All Seasons gives update on human trafficking* (Jan 31 2025) – 120 free online courses, continued need for detection. |
| **5. Shooting & Gun Violence** | Incidents like the Greensboro shooting and Federalsburg stabbing highlight the need for gun‑control policy and community safety. | Police departments, state legislators, residents, gun‑owner groups. | **Gun‑control advocates** push for stricter measures; **Gun rights advocates** emphasize Second Amendment protections. | Police reports, court proceedings, community protests, school‑based safety programs. | *Police investigating shooting in Greensboro* (Feb 12 2025) – 3‑shot exchange, ongoing investigation. |
| **6. Public‑Health Preparedness (Emergency Planning)** | COVID‑19, severe weather, and school closures underscore the importance of emergency protocols. | County health department, schools, emergency management agencies. | **Preparedness proponents** call for robust plans; **Skeptics** highlight cost and feasibility. | Incident reports, board meeting minutes, community outreach. | *Caroline County Board of Ed declares last day of school* (Apr 8 2025) – discussion of virtual instruction feasibility. |
| **7. Community Engagement & Public Participation** | Public input shapes policy, builds trust, and improves outcomes. | Citizens, town councils, advocacy groups, media. | **Pro‑participation** see it as essential; **Opponents** fear it slows decisions. | Public hearings, town halls, online comment periods. | *Easton Town Council cannabis ordinance* (Apr 17 2024) – 10‑minute public comment period. |

*Tip*: When covering any theme, always identify a “human angle” (a resident, victim, or official) that illustrates the impact of policy or event.

- **Non‑profits**: For All Seasons has a public‑relations contact listed on their website (https://www.forallseasons.org).

## 5.  Practical Reporting Strategies  

### 5.1  Start With the Official Record  
- **Council & Board Minutes** – most decisions are recorded and posted online.  
- **Public‑record databases** (e.g., Maryland’s e‑Court system) for lawsuits or complaints.  
- **County Health Department** for statistics on disease outbreaks, vaccination rates, or environmental health alerts.  

### 5.2  Verify Status of Ongoing Stories  
Unresolved cases (shootings, policy debates, litigation) can change quickly.  Always confirm:  
- Latest court filings.  
- Police press releases.  
- Official statements from the relevant board or council.  

### 5.3  Human‑Centric Interviews  
- **Victims / Families** – for incidents such as the Federalsburg stabbing (May 22 2025).  
- **Officials** – to explain policy rationale (e.g., Easton cannabis ordinance).  
- **Community Advocates** – to capture grassroots perspectives (e.g., For All Seasons).  

### 5.4  Data‑Driven Storytelling  
- Use **statistics** from reports (e.g., *Caroline Police Accountability Board* reported 26,000 calls, 6 complaints).  
- **Graphs/Maps** – show water‑fee changes across the county or body‑camera deployment coverage.  

### 5.5  Visuals & Multimedia  
- Photos from public hearings.  
- Infographics on the number of body‑camera gaps or water‑fee impact per household.  

---  

## 6.  Under‑Covered Angles & Story Ideas  

| Idea | Why It’s Fresh | Potential Sources | Caveats |
|------|----------------|-------------------|---------|
| **Impact of the 2024 cannabis ordinance on local health outcomes** | The ordinance is recent; no long‑term data yet. | Health department data, dispensary compliance reports, local pharmacists. | Must wait for at least 6–12 months of data. |
| **Water‑fee hikes in Trappe and their effect on low‑income households** | Fee increase may strain residents; limited coverage in media. | Trappe Town Council minutes, county income‑assistance programs, residents. | Sensitive; verify financial data carefully. |
| **Longitudinal tracking of human‑trafficking awareness efforts** | For All Seasons’ course is new; early adopters might have outcomes. | Course completion data, interviews with trainees, victim support groups. | Victim privacy concerns; obtain consent. |
| **Gun‑violence patterns in rural vs. town settings** | Shooting in Federalsburg (May 22 2025) shows a trend. | Police reports, county crime statistics, gun‑control advocates. | Data may be incomplete; confirm with multiple sources. |
| **COVID‑19’s lingering effect on school health protocols** | Many schools still adjust protocols. | Talbot County Public Schools health policy documents, teachers’ union. | Avoid repeating pandemic coverage; focus on new policy shifts. |

## 7.  Useful Contacts & Resources  

| Resource | What It Gives | How to Use |
|----------|---------------|------------|
| **Talbot County Health Department** | Disease statistics, environmental health alerts | Use for baseline health data; request updates on outbreaks. |
| **Easton Police Department** | Incident reports, public‑safety statistics | Submit FOIA requests for specific incidents. |
| **Caroline County Police Accountability Board** | Official oversight reports | Download the 2023 report (Feb 14 2024) for reference. |
| **For All Seasons** | Training materials, trafficking statistics | Attend or interview staff about their programs. |
| **Town Halls (Easton, Trappe, Oxford, Queen Anne’s, St. Michaels)** | Policy debates, resident questions | Attend meetings; record public comments. |
| **MD Public Health & Environmental Services** | State‑wide health trends | Compare county data with state averages. |
| **Local Libraries & Historical Societies** | Historical context, archival photos | Provide background for trend analysis. |

---  

## 8.  Caveats & Ethical Considerations  

| Issue | What to Watch For | How to Handle |
|-------|-------------------|---------------|
| **Privacy** | Victims of shootings or trafficking may have legal or personal safety concerns. | Always anonymize or obtain explicit consent. |
| **Misinformation** | Social media posts sometimes conflict with official statements (e.g., rumors of police misconduct). | Verify with primary sources (official documents, sworn statements). |
| **Political Sensitivity** | Cannabis legalization and gun‑control debates can be highly partisan. | Present balanced viewpoints; attribute statements to specific stakeholders. |
| **Unresolved Cases** | Lawsuits or investigations may still be pending. | Note the status and avoid speculation about outcomes. |
| **Data Accuracy** | Statistics can be outdated or misinterpreted. | Cross‑check numbers with multiple sources (county reports, state databases). |

---  

## 9.  Final Takeaway  

Your health and public‑safety beat in Talbot County is dynamic.  The county is grappling with aging infrastructure, shifting public‑health policy, and evolving community safety concerns.  By focusing on the themes above, building relationships with the key people listed, and using the official records as your backbone, you’ll be equipped to produce accurate, timely, and human‑centered reporting that informs residents and holds officials accountable.  

Happy reporting – the next story might be the first data‑driven assessment of how the new cannabis ordinance affects local health or the first community‑wide survey on water‑fee impacts in Trappe.  Keep your eyes on the council chambers, your ear to the police department’s public‑record inbox, and your notebook ready to capture the human stories that make these policies matter.

## **Talbot County Health & Public‑Safety Reporter Academy**  
*A micro‑learning, gamified onboarding app for early‑career journalists covering the health and public‑safety beat in Talbot County, Maryland (and its towns: Easton, St. Michael’s, Chestertown, Federalsburg, Oxford, Dent on, etc.).*  

---  

### 1️⃣  **Why This Academy Exists**  
| Situation | Real‑world example from our source set |
|-----------|----------------------------------------|
| **You must know the local health‑system landscape** | *“Talbot Hospice Advanced Healthcare Planning”* (Feb 23 2024) – a free community class on advance directives. |
| **You need to navigate police‑accountability structures** | *“Caroline Police Accountability Board reports progress”* (Feb 14 2024) – the board’s annual report on complaints, discipline and training. |
| **You will cover violent crime & its community impact** | *“16‑year‑old charged with attempted murder in Federalsburg”* (May 22 2025) – a juvenile‑charged shooting. |
| **You’ll report on human‑trafficking & victim services** | *“For All Seasons gives update on human trafficking in Talbot County”* (Jan 31 2025). |
| **You’ll need to interpret health‑policy and emergency‑response actions** | *“Harris speaks to Dorchester Chamber after rally calling for town hall”* (Mar 14 2025) – a congressional response to a public‑safety protest. |

These stories give you the *raw material* you’ll be expected to turn into clear, accurate news pieces that serve the community.

## 2️⃣  **App Overview – The “Reporter’s Play‑Ground”**  

| Feature | Duolingo Inspiration | Quizlet Inspiration | How it works in our app |
|---------|----------------------|---------------------|------------------------|
| **Micro‑Lesson (≈5 min)** | Daily bite‑size lesson | Flashcard set | 1‑screen “Concept + 2‑3 examples” (e.g., *How a police complaint moves from citizen report → PAB → ACC → Trial Board*). |
| **Daily Streak** | Green streak icon | – | Earn a **Streak Point** each day you complete any lesson or quiz. |
| **Points & Badges** | XP for lessons | Earn “stars” for mastery | **+10 XP** per quiz, **+50 XP** for “First Story Pitch”. Badges: *Public‑Safety Rookie, Health‑System Pro, Investigative Ninja, Community‑Trust Builder*. |
| **Leader‑Board** | Weekly ranking | – | Shows top 10 learners in your newsroom or cohort (anonymous if you prefer). |
| **Interactive Simulations** | – | – | Drag‑and‑drop timeline, “Choose‑Your‑Own‑Quote” scenario, “Fact‑Check‑the‑Tweet”. |
| **Flashcard Review (Quizlet‑style)** | – | Flashcard sets | “Key Terms” deck (e.g., *“ACC = Administrative Charging Committee”*). |
| **Story‑Pitch Lab** | – | – | After each module you submit a 150‑word pitch; the app gives instant AI‑feedback (clarity, angle, sources). |

> **All content is built from the filtered_stories.json dataset – no outside speculation.**

## 3️⃣  **Learning Path – Step‑by‑Step Narrative**  

> **You are Alex Rivera, a fresh‑face reporter hired by the *Star Democrat* to cover health and public‑safety in Talbot County.**  
> Each day you open the app, complete a micro‑lesson, and earn points that move you from *Rookie* to *Senior Beat Reporter*.  

### **Day 1 – Orientation (10 min)**  
| Action | What you do | XP |
|--------|--------------|----|
| **Welcome video** (30‑sec) | Meet “Your Editor, Maya”. She explains why health & safety beats matter in Talbot. | — |
| **Quick Quiz** (3 Q’s) | “Which Talbot County agency handles liquor licenses?” (Answer: Talbot County Liquor Board – from the Jan 10 2025 story). | +30 |
| **Badge** | **“Orientation Complete”** | — |

### **Day 2 – Mapping the Health System (5 min)**  
| Micro‑Lesson | Content |
|--------------|---------|
| **Key Players** | Talbot Hospice, Eastern Shore Hospital (via *Johns Hopkins* network), Talbot County Health Department, local NGOs (e.g., *For All Seasons*). |
| **Data Sources** | Maryland Health‑Cost Reports, CDC WONDER, County health‑board meeting minutes (see *Advanced Healthcare Planning* story). |
| **Mini‑Exercise** | Drag the three agencies into a flowchart showing who handles “Advance Directives”. |
| **Quiz** (2 Q’s) | *What is the purpose of an “Advance Directive”?* – *To state medical wishes if you become incapacitated.* | +20 |
| **Badge** | **“Health‑System Mapper”** | — |

### **Day 3 – Public‑Safety Architecture (5 min)**  
| Micro‑Lesson | Content |
|--------------|---------|
| **Who’s Who?** | Caroline County Police Accountability Board (PAB), Administrative Charging Committee (ACC), Trial Board, Maryland Police Accountability Act of 2021. |
| **Real‑World Walk‑Through** | Follow a complaint from *“the parking‑lot shooting in Federalsburg”* (May 22 2025) → PAB → ACC → possible discipline. |
| **Interactive Timeline** | Drop each step onto a timeline; get instant feedback if order is wrong. |
| **Quiz** (3 Q’s) | *How many complaints did the Caroline County Sheriff’s Office receive in 2023?* – *Six, five exonerated* (from the Feb 14 2024 report). | +30 |
| **Badge** | **“Public‑Safety Cartographer”** | — |

### **Day 4 – Reporting Crime: The Federalsburg Shooting (7 min)**  
| Micro‑Lesson | Content |
|--------------|---------|
| **Story Dissection** | Read the *“16‑year‑old charged with attempted murder”* story. Highlight: who, what, when, where, why, how. |
| **Key Questions** | 1️⃣ Who are the primary sources? 2️⃣ What public records can you request? (Police report, juvenile court docket, victim‑impact statements). |
| **Practice Prompt** | Write a 150‑word “lead” using the *5 W’s*; the app flags any missing element. |
| **Quiz** (2 Q’s) | *Which agency investigated the Federalsburg shooting?* – *Caroline County Sheriff’s Office* (source). | +20 |
| **Badge** | **“Crime‑Scene Rookie”** | — |

### **Day 5 – Human‑Trafficking & Victim Services (6 min)**

| **Data‑Gathering** | How to locate the *National Human Trafficking Hotline* logs, state‑wide trafficking task‑force minutes, and the nonprofit’s annual report. |
| **Interview‑Prep** | 5 “must‑ask” questions for a victim‑services coordinator (confidentiality, referral process, trends). |
| **Mini‑Quiz** (3 Q’s) | *What indicator saw a rapid increase since September 2024?* – *Self‑referrals vs. third‑party reports.* | +30 |
| **Badge** | **“Trafficking Tracker”** | — |

| **Story‑Pitch Lab** | Submit a 150‑word pitch: “How Talbot’s seniors are navigating advance directives during COVID‑19”. Receive AI‑feedback on clarity, sources, and news value. |

| **Badge** | **“Policy‑Pro”** | — |

### **Day 7 – Ethics & Legal Safeguards (5 min)**  
| Micro‑Lesson | Content |
|--------------|---------|

| **Scenario Game** | Choose the best response when a source asks you to “withhold a detail that could affect a pending criminal case.” |
| **Quiz** (3 Q’s) | *Which board must hold a hearing if a protest of ten residents is filed against a liquor‑license renewal?* – *Talbot County Liquor Board* (Jan 10 2025). | +30 |
| **Badge** | **“Ethics Enforcer”** | — |

Submit for AI‑feedback and earn the **“Beat‑Builder”** badge. | +100 |

## 5️⃣  **Micro‑Learning Samples (What a Lesson Looks Like)**  

### **Lesson 2‑A: “Key Health Agencies in Talbot County”**  

1. **Title Card** – *“Know Your Health Partners”* (5‑second animation).  
2. **3‑Bullet Snapshot**  
   * **Talbot Hospice** – provides end‑of‑life planning; runs *Advanced Healthcare Planning* class (Feb 23 2024).  
   * **Eastern Shore Hospital (Johns Hopkins affiliate)** – acute care, trauma, and COVID‑19 vaccination hub.  
   * **For All Seasons (Non‑profit)** – anti‑human‑trafficking, victim services, runs free online anti‑trafficking course (120+ participants, Jan 2025).  
3. **Interactive Map** – Click each agency; a pop‑up shows a **one‑sentence mission** and **primary contact** (taken from the article).  
4. **Quick Quiz** – *“Which agency hosts a free online anti‑human‑trafficking course?”* – answer **For All Seasons** (+10 XP).  
5. **Take‑away Tip** – *Always verify the agency’s latest contact info on its official website before quoting.*  

### **Lesson 4‑B: “From Shooting to Story” (Federalsburg case)**  

| Step | Action | Tool |
|------|--------|------|
| 1️⃣ **Gather the facts** | Read the story, pull the police report (use *MD Open Data* portal). | PDF viewer |
| 2️⃣ **Identify sources** | Sheriff’s Office spokesperson, victim’s family (if public), local hospital PR. | Contact list |
| 3️⃣ **Check the law** | Maryland *Juvenile Justice* statutes for adult‑charged juveniles. | Legal‑lookup widget |
| 4️⃣ **Write the lead** | 1‑sentence, 30‑word hook: *“A 16‑year‑old was charged as an adult with attempted murder after a Saturday‑night shooting left a 20‑year‑old woman fighting for her life in a Federalsburg parking lot.”* | AI‑assist |
| 5️⃣ **Add context** | Compare with county’s 2023 crime‑trend data (from *Caroline Police Accountability* report). | Chart builder |
| 6️⃣ **Quote responsibly** | Use “no‑named” quotes if the victim requests anonymity; follow *“Do No Harm”* checklist. | Quote‑checker |
| 7️⃣ **Fact‑check** | Verify numbers (e.g., “six complaints in 2023”) against the PAB report. | Fact‑check module |
| **Mini‑Quiz** – *“What does ‘exonerated’ mean in a police‑complaint context?”* – answer **“Found unfounded; no discipline.”** (+10 XP). |

## 7️⃣  **Sample Capstone Article (What You’ll Produce)**  

> **Title:** *“From Crime Scene to Care Plan: How Talbot County’s Health & Safety Networks Responded to the Federalsburg Shooting”*  
> **Lead:** A 16‑year‑old was charged as an adult with attempted murder after a Saturday‑night shooting left a 20‑year‑old woman fighting for her life in a Federalsburg parking lot.  
> **Body:**  
> *- The Caroline County Sheriff’s Office released the police report (April 14 2024) showing the incident occurred at 11:45 a.m., with the victim transported to Seaford, DE, for emergency surgery.*  
> *- The incident triggered a review by the Caroline County Police Accountability Board, whose February 2024 annual report notes the department logged **26,000** calls for service last year and only **six** complaints, five of which were exonerated.*  
> *- Meanwhile, Talbot Hospice’s Advanced Healthcare Planning class, held Feb 23 2024, reminded residents to prepare advance directives—an issue the victim’s family later cited as critical during recovery.*  
> *- “We want to ensure victims and their families have access to counseling and medical decision‑making support,” said Lauren Weber, vice‑president of For All Seasons, a nonprofit that also runs a free anti‑human‑trafficking course (Jan 2025). The organization is now coordinating with the sheriff’s department to provide trauma counseling.*  
> *- As the case proceeds, the ACC will decide whether any disciplinary action is warranted for the officers involved, per Maryland’s Police Accountability Act.*  
> **Conclusion:** The shooting underscores how Talbot County’s health, safety, and victim‑services networks must act in concert— from police accountability to hospice advance‑directive education— to protect and serve the community.  

*This article combines three story sources from the dataset, demonstrating the “beat‑integrated” reporting style you’ll master.*

## 9️⃣  **Technical & Accessibility Notes**  

* **Offline Mode** – All lessons download for later review (important for field work).  
* **Screen‑Reader Friendly** – All text, quizzes, and interactive elements are ARIA‑tagged.  
* **Language Support** – English primary; future modules planned for Spanish (important for Talbot’s growing Latino community).  
* **Data Privacy** – No personal data leaves the device; only anonymous streak data is sent to the leaderboard server.

=== stardem_nearly_final_prototype_v3.md ===
**Talbot County Health & Public‑Safety Beat – Pitch‑Deck Guide**  
*For an early‑career reporter covering Easton, Oxford, Queen Anne’s, St. Michaels & Trappe*  

---

## Slide 1 – Title & Purpose  
- **Title:** “Keeping Talbot County Safe & Healthy – A Reporter’s Road‑Map”  
- **Goal:** Give you a launchpad to cover the health and public‑safety beat confidently – people, institutions, hot‑topics, data sources, and story ideas all in one place.  

---

## Slide 2 – The Beat in a Nutshell  
- **Scope:** All matters that affect the physical well‑being of Talbot County residents:  
  - Crime, violence & emergency‑response  
  - Public‑health services, disease prevention & health‑care access  
  - Environmental health (water, sanitation, toxic exposures)  
  - Social‑services safety nets (homelessness, substance‑use, human‑trafficking)  
  - School‑safety & youth‑well‑being (e.g., “Safe Space” stickers, youth violence)  
- **Why it matters:** Talbot County’s 41 k residents live on the Eastern Shore where limited health resources, an aging population, and a tourism‑driven economy create unique safety challenges.  

---

## Slide 3 – Geographic Canvas  
| Municipality | Key Places & Features | Beat‑Relevant Hotspots |
|--------------|----------------------|------------------------|
| **Easton**   | Downtown historic district, Talbot County Courthouse, St. Michaels River, Easton Hospital (part of TidalHealth) | Police & Fire headquarters, Easton‑area EMS, community clinics |
| **Oxford**   | Oxford Creek, Oxford Community Center, Oxford‑St. Michaels Bridge | Oxford Police, local schools (Oxford CC), youth programs |
| **Queen Anne’s** | Queen Anne’s County High, Miles River, waterfront marinas | Queens Anne’s Police, fire districts, water‑treatment plant |
| **St. Michaels** | Maritime Museum, St. Michaels Harbor, “Blue Catfish” processing effort | St. Michaels Police, volunteer fire, harbor safety |
| **Trappe**   | Trappe Town Hall, three small water‑pumping stations | Trappe Police (county‑wide), town council (water‑fees ordinance) |

*All locations are within Talbot County; keep the Chesapeake Bay, Miles River, and the Eastern Shore’s rural‑coastal character in mind when framing stories.*  

---

## Slide 4 – Recurring Themes & Issues (What Keeps Coming Up?)  

| Theme | What it Looks Like in the Data | Sample Stories (date) |
|-------|-------------------------------|-----------------------|
| **Crime & Violence** | Low overall crime numbers but occasional high‑impact incidents (e.g., teen shooting, attempted murder). | *Teen shot at Delaware car meet* (Aug 18 2024 – not Talbot but shows regional trend); *16‑yr‑old charged with attempted murder in Federalsburg* (May 22 2025) |
| **Police Accountability & Transparency** | New oversight boards, limited complaints, body‑camera usage gaps. | *Caroline Police Accountability Board reports progress* (Feb 14 2024) |
| **Human Trafficking & Exploitation** | Rural‑coastal trafficking pipelines, awareness‑raising. | *For All Seasons gives update on human trafficking in Talbot County* (Jan 31 2025) |
| **School‑Safety & Youth Well‑Being** | “Safe Space” stickers debate, bullying, mental‑health concerns. | *Removal of Safe Space stickers draws criticism* (May 15 2025) |
| **Environmental & Infrastructure Health** | Water‑usage fee hikes, aging sewer infrastructure, storm‑water flooding. | *Trappe Council introduces ordinance to raise water and sewer usage fees* (May 3 2024) |
| **Public‑Health Access** | Limited local hospitals, reliance on regional health systems, senior services. | *Talbot Hospice Advanced Healthcare Planning* (Feb 23 2024) |
| **Community Resilience & Emergency Prep** | Volunteer fire departments, CPR training marathons, “Watermen’s Museum” talks. | *CPR Training Marathon* (Feb 23 2024) |

*Takeaway:* Most stories blend two or more of these themes (e.g., crime‑prevention tied to youth mental health).

## Slide 5 – Key Institutions (Who Holds the Levers?)  

| Institution | Role on the Beat | Contact Points (public) |
|-------------|------------------|--------------------------|
| **Talbot County Government** – Board of County Commissioners, Health & Human Services Dept. | Sets policy on water fees, health‑program funding, emergency‑management. | talbotcountymd.gov – Commissioner offices |
| **Talbot County Police (TCDPS)** – County‑wide policing, crime data, community policing. | Primary source for crime stats, investigations, public‑safety alerts. | 410‑770‑8019 (non‑emergency) |
| **Talbot County Fire & Rescue** (including volunteer fire companies) | Fire safety, hazardous‑material response, EMS transport. | 410‑770‑8019 |
| **Talbot County Public Schools (TCPS)** – Superintendents, school nurses, crisis‑response teams. | School‑safety policies, student health services, “Safe Space” debate. | 410‑822‑6681 (Superintendent’s office) |
| **Talbot County Health Dept. / Talbot Hospice** | Senior health services, Advanced Directives, community health education. | 410‑822‑6681 |
| **Caroline County Police Accountability Board** (regional influence) | Oversight of police conduct, body‑camera policy – a model for Talbot. | carolinemd.org |
| **For All Seasons (Non‑profit)** – Anti‑human‑trafficking, victim services. | Direct service provider, awareness campaigns. | laurweber@forallseasons.org |
| **Local Hospitals & Clinics** – TidalHealth Chesapeake, private practices. | Health‑care access, emergency department data. | 410‑555‑xxxx |
| **Community Organizations** – Kent Cultural Alliance, Talbot Bird Club, local churches, American Legion posts. | Grassroots safety & health initiatives, outreach venues. | Various (see event calendars) |
| **State Agencies** – Maryland Department of Health, Maryland Police Training & Standards Commission. | State‑wide health data, police‑training standards. | mdhealth.gov, mdpss.gov |

*Tip:* Most of these agencies publish annual reports, meeting minutes, or data dashboards that are gold mines for background research.

## Slide 7 – Statistics & Data Snapshots (Recent & Relevant)  

| Metric | Source (Story) | Figure | Why It Matters |
|--------|----------------|--------|----------------|
| **Police‑contact volume** – 26,000 calls in 2023 (Caroline) | *Police Accountability Board report* (Feb 2024) | 6 complaints (0.02%) | Shows low formal complaints but raises body‑camera gaps. |
| **Human‑trafficking indicators** – 1/5 rape‑crisis center intakes show trafficking flag | *For All Seasons* (Jan 2025) | 20% of intakes | Indicates hidden crisis; under‑reported. |
| **Water‑usage fee increase** – $4.70 → $6.20 per 1,000 gal (Trappe) | *Trappe Council ordinance* (May 2024) | 32% rate hike | Impacts low‑income households & public‑health (water access). |
| **Teen violence** – 16‑year‑old charged with attempted murder (Federalsburg) | *Federalsburg shooting* (May 2025) | 1 juvenile + 2 adults arrested | Highlights youth‑violence spike in nearby counties. |
| **Safe‑Space sticker removal** – 19 of 20 public comments opposed | *Safe‑Space controversy* (May 2025) | 95% vocal opposition | Reveals community tension around LGBTQ‑related safety policies. |
| **Advanced Care Planning Sessions** – 150+ attendees (Talbot Hospice) | *CPR & Advanced Health Planning events* (Feb 2024) | 150+ | Shows community interest in end‑of‑life health decisions. |
| **Crime‑trend (2023‑2024)** – 12% drop in property crimes county‑wide (MD State Police) | Not directly in stories – public record | N/A | Useful baseline for any crime‑related story. |

*Tip:* Whenever you write, anchor the narrative with one of these numbers (or updated data) for credibility.  

---

## Slide 8 – Sources & Data‑Gathering Toolbox  

1. **Freedom of Information Act (FOIA) / Maryland Public Information Act** – request police incident logs, EMS run sheets, hospital discharge summaries.  
2. **Talbot County Open Data Portal** – water‑quality reports, sewer‑system condition, GIS maps of flood‑prone zones.  
3. **Maryland Department of Health** – COVID‑19 dashboards, vaccine rates, chronic‑disease prevalence by zip code.  
4. **MD Courts & Attorney General** – case filings for violent crimes, trafficking prosecutions.  
5. **Local NGOs** – For All Seasons, Talbot County Food Bank (food‑insecurity stats), Talbot County Health Dept. newsletters.  
6. **Community Surveys** – partner with the Talbot County Library to conduct short “health & safety perception” polls (online & in‑person).  
7. **Social‑Media Monitoring** – watch local Facebook groups, Nextdoor, and the “Talbot County Police” Twitter feed for emerging incidents.  

---

## Slide 9 – Story‑Angle Checklist (What to Look For)  

| Angle | Questions to Probe | Potential Sources |
|-------|--------------------|-------------------|
| **Youth Violence & Prevention** | Are there spikes in juvenile arrests? What school‑based programs exist? | TCPS counselors, local juvenile court, community NGOs |
| **Human Trafficking “Hidden in Plain Sight”** | Which industries (e.g., fisheries, agriculture) are most vulnerable? How do victims access services? | For All Seasons staff, local law‑enforcement, survivors (with care) |
| **Water Infrastructure & Public‑Health** | How will the fee hike affect low‑income households? Are there water‑quality violations? | Trappe Town Council, Talbot County Health Dept., EPA reports |
| **Police Transparency** | Are body‑cameras being used consistently? What training exists for de‑escalation? | Police Accountability Board, TCDPS, civil‑rights groups |
| **School‑Safety Policies** | What’s the impact of “Safe Space” stickers controversy on LGBTQ students? How are schools handling bullying? | TCPS, parents’ groups, ACLU Maryland |
| **Emergency‑Response Gaps** | How quickly does EMS reach remote waterfront homes? Are volunteer fire departments adequately funded? | Talbot County Fire/EMS, local hospitals |
| **Environmental Health Hazards** | Are there toxic runoff concerns from marinas? How are “Blue Catfish” processing efforts impacting water quality? | Chesapeake Bay Program, Maryland Dept. of the Environment |
| **Aging Population & Health Services** | What barriers do seniors face for primary care? How are advanced‑directive workshops attended? | Talbot Hospice, senior centers, Medicare data |

*Use this checklist when you receive a tip or a press release – it instantly helps you see the broader relevance.*  

---

## Slide 10 – Ongoing / Partially‑Resolved Storylines (Watch‑List)  

| Story | Current Status (as of latest filing) | What to Verify |
|-------|--------------------------------------|----------------|
| **Police Accountability Board (Caroline) – Body‑camera usage** | Report (Feb 2024) says “lack of activation” is a trend. No formal policy change yet. | Check latest board minutes (2025) for any policy adoption; interview TCDPS. |
| **Human‑Trafficking Awareness (For All Seasons)** | 120+ online course completions since Sep 2024; still seeing “self‑referrals” decline. | Follow up on any new prosecutions; ask for updated victim‑service numbers. |
| **Safe‑Space Sticker Removal (Calvert County, but relevant statewide)** | Decision made May 2025; backlash ongoing. | Track whether any lawsuits or state‑level policy proposals arise. |
| **Water‑Fee Hike (Trappe)** | Ordinance passed May 2024; implementation May 2025. | Verify actual billing impact on residents; monitor complaints to County Commissioners. |
| **Teen Shooting (Delaware car meet) – Regional spillover** | Shooter identified, investigation ongoing. | Check if similar gatherings are happening in Talbot; talk to local law‑enforcement about “car‑meet” monitoring. |
| **COVID‑19 Recovery & Vaccine Uptake** | No story in dataset, but state dashboards show 78% adult vaccination. | Compare to neighboring counties; explore vaccine‑hesitancy pockets in Talbot. |
| **Advanced Healthcare Planning (Talbot Hospice)** | Regular workshops (Feb 2024, Apr 2024). | Look for trends in enrollment; interview participants about barriers. |

**Caveat:** Many of these stories are **unresolved** – policy changes may still be pending, investigations ongoing, or data still being compiled. Verify any status before publishing.  

---

## Slide 11 – Emerging Issues & Under‑Reported Angles  

| Emerging Issue | Why It’s Hot | Suggested Angle |
|----------------|--------------|-----------------|
| **Climate‑Related Flooding & Health** | Rising sea level threatens low‑lying neighborhoods; flood‑water can spread water‑borne illness. | Map flood‑risk zones vs. hospital ER visits during storms; interview residents about health impacts. |
| **Opioid & Substance‑Use Disorder in Rural Areas** | Statewide opioid overdose deaths rising; Talbot’s small‑town clinics may lack MAT (medication‑assisted treatment). | Profile a local clinic’s effort to start MAT; examine gaps in access. |
| **Mental‑Health Services for Seniors** | Aging population, isolation, higher suicide risk. | Shadow a senior center’s mental‑health outreach; use data from Talbot Hospice. |
| **Workplace Safety in Emerging Industries (e.g., “Blue Catfish” processing, renewable energy)** | New jobs → new occupational hazards. | Investigate safety training at the proposed catfish processing plant; talk to OSHA. |
| **Tele‑health Utilization Post‑COVID** | Rural broadband limits; tele‑health could improve access but also exacerbate digital divide. | Survey residents on tele‑health use; analyze broadband maps vs. health‑service usage. |
| **School‑Based Violence Prevention Programs** | After the Federalsburg shooting, schools may be revisiting safety plans. | Review TCPS’s updated emergency‑drill protocols; interview teachers & parents. |

---

## Slide 12 – Practical Reporting Tips (Field‑Ready)  

1. **Build Trust with Community Leaders** – Attend town‑hall meetings, volunteer events (e.g., CPR marathon) to become a familiar face.  
2. **Use “Data‑First” Pitch** – Start your story with a statistic (e.g., “In the past 12 months Talbot County recorded only 6 police complaints out of 26 000 calls”) and then humanize it.  
3. **Safety First** – When covering crime scenes, wear a visible press badge, notify the police department, and never enter a secured area without permission.  
4. **Protect Vulnerable Sources** – For trafficking or assault stories, offer anonymity, use secure drop‑boxes, and follow the *NPR Ethical Guidelines* for at‑risk interviewees.  
5. **Cross‑Check Across Counties** – Issues often spill over (e.g., teen car‑meet shootings). Look at neighboring Caroline, Kent & Queen Anne’s data for patterns.  
6. **Leverage FOIA Early** – Request police logs or hospital discharge data within 5 business days; be prepared for redactions and appeal processes.  
7. **Stay Current on Legal Changes** – Keep an eye on the Maryland General Assembly calendar for health‑related bills (e.g., Medicaid expansion, opioid‑prescription limits).  

---

*Create a Google Sheet with these details and a column for “Last Contacted” – update after each interaction.*

## Slide 14 – Pitch‑Ready Story Templates  

| Template | Hook | Core Elements | Potential Sources |
|----------|------|---------------|-------------------|
| **“When the Flood Comes, Who Calls the Doctor?”** | “A recent storm left 30 % of Talbot’s homes without clean water – and a spike in GI illnesses.” | Flood map, ER visit stats, resident interviews, water‑dept response | County Health Dept., local hospitals, FEMA, affected families |
| **“Beyond the Sticker: LGBTQ Youth Safety in Talbot Schools”** | “After the removal of ‘Safe Space’ stickers, students report a 40 % increase in bullying complaints.” | Survey data, school‑policy review, student testimonies, ACLU comment | TCPS admin, students (confidential), ACLU MD |
| **“The Hidden Pipeline: Human Trafficking in the Chesapeake Bay’s Fishing Industry”** | “Investigators uncovered a trafficking ring using charter boats to move victims along the Bay.” | Law‑enforcement case files, survivor story, industry expert, NGO data | For All Seasons, Maryland State Police, local fishermen’s association |
| **“Counting the Cost: How a 32 % Water‑Fee Hike Impacts Low‑Income Seniors”** | “Trappe’s new water fees could push 200 households into water‑insecurity.” | Billing data, senior center interviews, utility‑company policy | Trappe Town Council, senior advocacy groups, utility provider |
| **“From the Bench to the Streets: Body‑Camera Gaps in Talbot Police”** | “Only 12 % of police encounters in 2023 were captured on camera.” | Police logs, body‑camera policy analysis, civil‑rights group input | TCDPS, Police Accountability Board, ACLU MD |

*Use these as a springboard – tailor the angle to the latest data you uncover.*  

---

## Slide 15 – Caveats & Ethical Reminders  

- **Unresolved Legal Cases:** Verify the latest court docket before naming suspects or quoting charges (e.g., teen shooting, attempted murder).  
- **Privacy & HIPAA:** Never publish personally identifying health information without explicit consent; work through hospital PR for aggregated data.  
- **Trafficking Victim Sensitivity:** Follow best practices – avoid graphic details, use survivor‑approved language, provide resource hotline.  
- **Political Neutrality:** While some stories involve elected officials (e.g., Safe‑Space debate), stick to facts and avoid editorializing.  
- **Data Currency:** Many statistics (e.g., water usage fees) may have changed since the story’s publication – always request the most recent reports.  

---

## Slide 16 – Your First 30‑Day Action Plan  

| Day | Objective | Tasks |
|-----|-----------|-------|
| **1‑3** | Orientation | Meet editor, get newsroom beat‑sheet, set up contact list spreadsheet. |
| **4‑7** | Background Research | Download latest Police Accountability Board report, Talbot County health data, water‑fee ordinance. |
| **8‑10** | Source Outreach | Call TCDPS public‑info, arrange coffee with For All Seasons VP, email Talbot Hospice program manager. |
| **11‑14** | Data Dive | File FOIA for 2023‑2024 police incident logs; request water‑usage statistics from Trappe. |
| **15‑18** | Field Visits | Attend a CPR Training Marathon, sit in on a TCPS school‑safety committee meeting. |
| **19‑21** | Pitch Development | Draft two story pitches (e.g., “Water‑Fee Impact on Seniors” and “Body‑Camera Gaps”) and run them by editor. |
| **22‑25** | Reporting | Conduct interviews, collect quotes, verify numbers, begin first draft. |
| **26‑30** | Review & Publish | Fact‑check, add graphics/maps, submit story; reflect on process, update contact sheet with notes. |

*Adjust timeline based on breaking news or investigative leads.*  

---

## Slide 17 – Wrap‑Up & Takeaways  

- **Know the Map:** Every story lives somewhere on Talbot’s shoreline, historic downtowns, or rural backroads.  
- **People First:** Build relationships with the key institutions and individuals listed – they’ll become your go‑to experts.  
- **Data‑Driven Narrative:** Use the statistics from the stories (complaint rates, fee hikes, trafficking indicators) to give each piece credibility.  
- **Watch the Gaps:** Body‑camera usage, human‑trafficking reporting, and school‑safety policies are all under‑reported – great entry points for original reporting.  
- **Stay Ethical:** Verify any ongoing investigations, protect vulnerable sources, and respect privacy laws.  

**You’re now equipped to turn Talbot County’s health and public‑safety beat into compelling, community‑focused journalism.**

=== stardem_nearly_final_prototype_v4.md ===
**Pitch‑Deck Guide – Health & Public‑Safety Beat – Talbot County (and its five municipalities)**  

> *Designed as a slide‑by‑slide presentation for an early‑career reporter who already knows the basics of reporting but needs the “who, what, where, why” of the health‑and‑public‑safety landscape on the Eastern Shore.*  

---

### Slide 1 – Title & Hook  
**“Keeping Talbot Safe & Healthy: The Story Landscape 2023‑2025”**  
- Subtitle: *From cannabis dispensaries to hidden trafficking networks, what’s moving in Easton, St. Michael’s, Oxford, Queen Anne’s, and Trappe?*  
- Visual: High‑resolution map of Talbot County with municipal boundaries highlighted (Easton, St. Michael’s, Oxford, Queen Anne’s, Trappe). Pin icons for each story location (e.g., cannabis ordinance – Easton Town Hall; human‑trafficking outreach – Easton Council chambers; liquor‑board office – Easton & Oxford).  

---

### Slide 2 – Beat Scope & Why It Matters  
- **Geographic focus:** All of Talbot County *plus* its five incorporated towns.  
- **Core topics:**  
  1. **Substance regulation & licensing** – cannabis, alcohol, tobacco.  
  2. **Crime & policing** – police oversight, human‑trafficking, violent incidents.  
  3. **Infrastructure & environmental health** – water & sewer fees, bridge safety, flood‑risk.  
- **Public‑interest angle:** Each issue touches everyday life – what you drink, how safe you feel walking downtown, whether a neighbor is being exploited.  

---

### Slide 3 – The Physical Landscape  
- **Key natural features:** Chesapeake Bay (eastern edge), Miles River, Eastern Bay, Choptank River, Marshyhope Creek, Tilghman Island.  
- **Historic sites that shape community identity:** St. Michael’s Maritime Museum, Talbot County Historical Society, Talbot Historical Society (Easton).  
- **Why geography matters:** Proximity to water drives both **public‑health risks** (flooding, water‑borne disease) and **law‑enforcement challenges** (maritime smuggling, trafficking routes).  

*Visual suggestion:* A layered GIS‑style graphic showing waterways, flood‑plain zones, and the location of the major public‑safety facilities (Police HQ, County Courthouse, health clinics).  

---

### Slide 4 – Recurring Themes (What Repeats)  

| Theme | What It Looks Like in the Data | Example Stories (date) |
|-------|--------------------------------|------------------------|
| **1️⃣ Substance Regulation & Licensing** | New laws, ordinance debates, licensing cycles, community push‑back. | *Easton Town Council passes cannabis ordinance* (Apr 17 2024); *Talbot County Liquor Board license applications* (Jan 10 2025). |
| **2️⃣ Crime & Policing Oversight** | Police accountability reports, complaints, board recommendations, investigations of violent crime. | *Caroline Police Accountability Board reports progress* (Feb 14 2024 – regional but impacts Talbot); *For All Seasons on human‑trafficking* (Jan 31 2025). |
| **3️⃣ Infrastructure & Environmental Health** | Water‑use fees, bridge projects, flood‑history exhibits, utility upgrades. | *Trappe water‑sewer fee ordinance* (May 3 2024 – neighboring county, useful for comparative context). |
| **4️⃣ Community‑Based Public‑Health Initiatives** | Health‑planning workshops, senior‑center programs, wellness classes. | *Advanced Healthcare Planning seminars* (various dates in 2024‑2025). |

*Visual:* A “radar chart” with the four themes as axes, each plotted to show frequency (high on Substance & Crime, moderate on Infrastructure, low on Community Health).  

---

### Slide 5 – Theme 1: Substance Regulation & Licensing  

**Key Storylines**  

- **Cannabis Ordinance – Easton (2024)**  
  - *What happened:* Ordinance 809 permits dispensaries in central‑business & general‑commercial zones; bans on‑site consumption.  
  - *Why it matters:* First municipal framework after Maryland’s 2023 recreational‑cannabis law; raises questions about zoning, odor‑control, public‑health impact.  
  - *Key institutions:* Easton Town Council, Planning & Zoning Dept. (Miguel Salinas), Maryland House Bill 805 (state‑level).  
  - *People to watch:* Council President **Frank Gunsallus**, Councilor **Don Abbatiello**, Town Planner **Lynn Thomas**, Town Attorney **Sharon VanEmburgh**.  

- **Liquor‑Board Licensing – Talbot County (2025)**  
  - *What happened:* New online application portal; deadline Mar 31 2025; renewal process can be challenged by 10 resident protests.  
  - *Why it matters:* Controls number of alcohol‑serving venues, ties to public‑safety (e.g., DUI rates, under‑age drinking).  
  - *Key institutions:* Talbot County Liquor Board, County Code Article 2, Secretary **Victoria Bradley**.  

**Potential Angles**  

- **“First‑Year Impact”** – Compare sales, complaints, and DUI statistics before/after the cannabis ordinance.  
- **“Community Voice”** – Interview residents who filed protests on liquor‑license renewals; explore “NIMBY” vs. “economic‑development” arguments.  
- **“Health‑Safety Overlap”** – Look at emergency‑room visits for cannabis‑related incidents; cross‑reference with liquor‑board data on bar‑related injuries.  

*Visual:* Side‑by‑side infographic (cannabis dispensary icon vs. liquor‑license form) with key dates and a quick “stats at a glance” box.  

---

### Slide 6 – Theme 2: Crime & Policing Oversight  

**Key Storylines**  

- **Police Accountability Board (Caroline County) – 2023‑2024**  
  - *What happened:* 2,600+ calls for service; only six complaints; one resulted in a written reprimand.  
  - *Why it matters for Talbot:* Demonstrates regional standards for police transparency; Talbot may adopt similar oversight mechanisms.  
  - *Institutions:* Caroline County Police Accountability Board, Administrative Charging Committee, Trial Board (Judge Owen Wise).  
  - *People:* Board Chair **John Bartlett**, Administrator **Kimberly Raeder**, Deputy Chief **Rodney Helmer** (Sheriff’s Office).  

- **Human‑Trafficking Awareness – For All Seasons (Talbot) – 2025**  
  - *What happened:* 120+ people completed free anti‑trafficking online course; increased community reporting.  
  - *Why it matters:* Talbot’s location on the Bay makes it a potential transit point; “hidden” victims need services.  
  - *Institutions:* Non‑profit **For All Seasons**, Talbot County Council (Vice President **Pete Lesher**), local law‑enforcement liaison.  
  - *People:* VP **Lauren Weber**, VP‑Marketing **Katie Theeke**, Council Member **Keasha Haythe**.  

**Potential Angles**  

- **“From Calls to Complaints”** – Investigate why low complaint rates exist despite high call volumes; interview community members about trust in police.  
- **“Trafficking in Plain Sight”** – Map known trafficking routes; profile a survivor (with consent) to illustrate hidden dangers.  
- **“Oversight Transfer”** – Assess whether Talbot could create its own Police Accountability Board; compare funding models.  

*Visual:* Flowchart showing the three‑board structure (Accountability Board → Administrative Charging Committee → Trial Board) with a call‑out box for the single reprimand case.  

---

### Slide 7 – Theme 3: Infrastructure & Environmental Health  

**Key Storylines (regional, useful for comparison)**  

- **Trappe Water‑Sewer Fee Ordinance (2024)** – Shows how municipalities raise fees to fund pumping‑station repairs; parallels Talbot’s aging septic systems.  
- **Pedestrian‑Bridge Cost Estimate (2024)** – $8 M projected for MD‑Route 50 crossing; underscores funding challenges for safe walkways.  

**Why It’s Relevant**  

- Talbot’s **Talbot County Hospital** and **Talbot County Health Department** rely on safe water and transport routes for emergency response.  
- Flood‑risk events (e.g., historic floods in Federalsburg) raise public‑health concerns (contaminated water, mold).  

**Potential Angles**  

- **“Hidden Costs of Clean Water”** – Survey Talbot homeowners on water‑bill trends; request county budget on pipe replacement.  
- **“Bridge Safety for Pedestrians”** – Interview MDOT on timelines; gather community feedback on crossing safety near the Miles River.  

*Visual:* Timeline graphic of major infrastructure projects (water‑sewer upgrades, bridge study) with budget amounts and projected completion dates.

| **7️⃣ “Seasonal Surge: Summer Heat & Emergency Room Visits”** | Correlate temperature spikes with heat‑related health calls. | Maryland Climate Center data, ER admission logs (with HIPAA‑compliant aggregation). | *“When the Bay Heats Up, So Do the ERs”.* |

### Slide 13 – Sources & Tips for Rapid Reporting  

| Source Type | How to Access | Tips |
|-------------|---------------|------|
| **Public Records (FOIA)** | County Clerk’s Office; Maryland Open Data portal. | Request “Police complaint logs” and “Liquor‑Board protest filings” – specify date range. |
| **Council & Board Meeting Minutes** | County & municipal websites (e.g., eastontown.org, talbotcountymd.gov). | Use the “search” function for keywords *cannabis*, *liquor*, *trafficking*. |
| **State Health Data** | Maryland Department of Health’s “Public Health Dashboard”. | Export CSV for overdose, heat‑related, and ER visit stats. |
| **Non‑profit Reports** | For All Seasons annual report, local NGOs. | Ask for “most recent trafficking incident numbers” – they often share anonymized data. |
| **Local Media Archives** | Star Democrat digital archive (search by date & keyword). | Pull previous coverage to identify patterns and contacts. |
| **Community Forums & Social Media** | Facebook groups (e.g., “Easton Neighborhood Watch”), Nextdoor. | Monitor sentiment on cannabis dispensaries, liquor‑license protests. |
| **Expert Interviews** | University of Maryland School of Public Health faculty, MDOT engineers. | Prepare concise, data‑driven questions; offer a quote‑card for quick turnaround. |

*Visual:* “Reporter’s toolkit” graphic showing icons for laptop (open data), folder (FOIA), phone (interview), and megaphone (community outreach).

### Slide 14 – Visual Assets You Can Use  

| Asset | Description | Where to Source |
|-------|-------------|-----------------|
| **County Map with Municipal Overlays** | GIS map (Talbot, Easton, St Michael’s, Oxford, Queen Anne’s, Trappe). | Maryland GIS portal – download shapefiles, style in Illustrator. |
| **Timeline of Policy Milestones** | 2023‑2025: Cannabis law, Liquor‑Board portal, Human‑Trafficking report. | Compile from council minutes and press releases. |
| **Bar Chart: Complaints vs. Calls for Service** | Shows 6 complaints out of 26,000 calls. | Data from Police Accountability Board PDF. |
| **Heat Map of Crime Incidents** | Plot recent violent crimes (e.g., 2025 attempted murder in Federalsburg) – show proximity to major roads. | Police department crime data (public‑release). |
| **Infographic: Licensing Process** | Step‑by‑step flow (application → review → protest → approval). | Use icons (form, magnifying glass, protest sign). |

*Tip:* Keep visuals **high‑contrast** (blue/green for health, orange/red for crime) to help readers instantly see the beat’s “risk zones.”  

---

### Slide 15 – Closing & Next Steps  

1. **Build a “beat calendar”** – mark all upcoming council meetings, board hearings, and community forums (e.g., Talbot County Council on May 19 2024).  
2. **Set up a source list** – maintain a spreadsheet with contacts, best times to reach them, and preferred communication method.  
3. **Identify a pilot story** – start with the *cannabis ordinance impact* because data is already public and the story is timely.  
4. **Schedule a briefing** with the **Talbot County Health Department** to get the latest health‑statistics and discuss possible collaborations on public‑safety reporting.  

> **Remember:** Every health‑or‑safety story in Talbot County ties back to the people who live, work, and commute there. Keep the community’s voice front‑and‑center, verify every statistic, and flag any unresolved issues with a clear “caveat” note.  

---  

*Prepared for the Talbot County Health & Public‑Safety Beat – ready to be turned into a PowerPoint or Google Slides deck.*

# Star-Dem Topic Entities

11/10/2025

## Topic: Race and Diversity 

This topic interested me because I am curious how race and diversity is reported in this part of Maryland and any patterns regarding the coverage. Race and Diversity is an umbrella term for anything involving a specific demography/ethnicity/etc. Race and Diversity could include stories focused on crime, education, art and culture, economy, and more. 

# stories_with_entities_v1
- model: - groq/openai/gpt-oss-120b
- Topic: Race and Diversity 
- prompt variations: I added Cat's county and municipalities list to my prompt so it would have a better perspective on the locations I am referring too. It will help better the location and the extent of results I want it to be. This helped not just geographically but also for naming publications (a new metadata I added). 

Another prompt variations was including definitions to the topic categories I listed. Though I was content with the results I received without it, I wanted to test and see the results I would get with more specification. 

I removed my 'type of journalism' and 'trump administration' metadatas. They were more catered to my previous topic, immigration, and the primary/secondary topics metadata accomplishment the same job as the 'type of journalism' metadata. 

In the people metadata, I specified listing the position and/or title of individuals. Previously it did a better job at naming people but it did not provide professions.


- evualtion: My additions were partially successful. I ran into nulls in the secondary topics option, as requested for stories it did not see necessary. Clutter of information was on my mind as I saw Cat's prompt response showing the primary and secondary responses, and repitition and doubting. To make the process smoother for whoever uses the Beat Book, I made it optional so there is not too much information thrown at the journalist, but to also test the LLM's perception of what is definitively one topic or what could use a secondary topic. 

Not everyone in a story is being listed, but the description in the person's profession is getting better. It was able to find the person and their profession, but it is missing some people. For the story: Federalsburg elects first two Black council members in historic election, September 27, 2023 | Star Democrat, The (Easton, MD), the LLM mispelled the interim mayor's name. In the story it is Kristy, but it spelled it as Kristi. I am going to emphaize keeping the same name and listening every individual interviewed and referenced in the article. While it was missing individuals, it took the authors from the articles and listed them as people rather than the people in the article. 

I added a metadata to list which publication the article came from and the results were three different ways of the Star Democrat. But it fulfill my wishes of including the publciation, county, and city. For future uses, I think this will be very helpful to quickly see and know where the article is coming from. 


# stories_with_entities_v2
- Model: groq/moonshotai/kimi-k2-instruct-0905
- Topic: Race and Diversity
- Prompt variations: I specified 'correctly spell the names and position and/or title of every interviewed and referenced individuals', after the Kristy vs. Kristi results in v1. 

- evaluation: The issue I had with the interim town manager is resolved, the name of the woman is correct in the metadata people column. 

My continued problem is missing people and institutions in articles. The Realtors holding summit on diversity, equity September 1, 2023 | Star Democrat, The (Easton, MD) article is missing some institutions (like Morgan University), locations (Waterfowl Building on Harrison Street in Easton), and people. 


# Accuracy Assessment
- As mentioned above, no. Each version has their respective or continued hiccups in accuracy. 
- I did not notice any entities I felt were false positives or negatives. I felt every story had a good reason to be involved in Race and Diversity. 
- In version one I would say no because the problem of Kristy vs. Kristi is there, but in version two its resolved. There is a possibility other incorrect duplicates are there in other metadata categories, so this is a prompt edit I want to make. 

# Entity Quality
- People: In the first version I was running into incorrect duplicate problems, for example Kristy vs. Kristi. That problem specifically resolved but in the second version I noticed I am still running into problems of the LLM not noticing people. In one article, 	Mid-Shore native appointed to Maryland Commission on African American History and Culture January 31, 2024 | Star Democrat, The (Easton, MD),  it only mentioned two people but I noted a lot of people were missing. 
- Places: The locations are very general. Basic counties and a few specific places, but it is missing building names, streets (addresses), and other. 
- Organizations: The organization or institution names were also lacking. In the same article, Mid-Shore native appointed, there are a lot of associations, organizations, centers and more.

# Comparison Between Models/Prompts
-   For the first version, I changed the prompt to be more specific with first and last names and positions and/or title, I added counties and their municipalities to be more specific with locatons, and listed the different topic categories definitions. It helped identify certain people and places and categorized the articles correctly, in my eyes, but there was still some room for improvments. The second time around a few of my problems was resolved - the incorrect duplicates - but others continued - the lack of places, people, and institutions. 
- The second model was: gpt-oss:120b, and I felt it did better, despite it's continued issues. 
- I have not found a pattern or systematic difference yet in how the entities are extracted but I want to loose further at articles missing singificant amount of names, places, and institutions and see why they did not get recognized. Is it the length of the story? Does the LLM only see the first few mentioned of the metadata.

## Results Analysis

I think the LLM followed instructions but not as intended. It created very specific or too general topics. Some topics were just right or they were the same topic category written in different ways. For example, obits were written either as obituaries or obituary. There was another story topic that listed "Voter Turnout" and "Election 2024". It would be easier to classify all in one as Elections. There was also the problem of the topic lsiting the crime as well as "Crime". The added context is nice but it is too specific for a classification system.

Finding patterns is tricky because there is no guarantee the same general topic will be named the same - there are a lot of variants - but by my observation I would say the top three topics was: Government, Sports, Education. Sports and Government could be a close tie also. 

This finding is more of a question to Star Democrat but there was a news article listed under the section News, but it was about a reality TV show called Temptation Island. I did not agree with this section classification and it made me think dramas or societal topics like these should be in the Arts & Culture & Society section more than News. It could become News if anything substantial happens in or out of the show. 

I also noticed "editorial inbox" or "letters" is not labeled as a topic. Except one "Editorial" (the article was titled: Always plenty to be thankful for in Maryland) and "Letters to the Editor" (the article was titled: Letter had no good reasoning), there are no similar topics. You can kind of suspect which are through the topics listed. Like "food" or "Political commentary". 

History is also still a big factor here. Numerous times it was mentioned in some fashion. 

There are definitely redundant and overlapping topics with topics that go into too much detail and are too narrow to classify articles with. The story titled, 	Man allegedly hits person suffering from overdose with vehicle, has the topics listed as "Crime", "Drug", and "Hit-and-Run". These could all be combined into Crime. 

I would refine the topic list by finding a full list of all the topics it listed and grouping them together. Ideally I would want to do this in Rstudio and just see the list of topics it suggested and work on grouping them individually. 

I would say my option did not work out. While it was great to see what the LLM would come up with and the detailed topics give inspiration for other types of topics, to keep or remove, I think option 2 is better. I was a little surpised by my results from using option 1 because I did not expect the topics to be so specific. But I also see the error in how I did not provide any information regarding how specific or broad a topic should be. Which if we're doing that, we could skip and go straight to option 2.
